fit the preferences expressed by   those selecting header fields, such as when an
origin server has   selected the response's language based on the request's   [Accept-Language](#section-5 "text-left") header field.[¶](#section-5 "text-left")

Vary might be elided when an origin server considers variance in   content selection
to be less significant than Vary's performance impact   on caching, particularly
when reuse is already limited by cache   response directives ([Section 5.2](#name-field-names "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-5.2
"text-left")]).[¶](#section-5.2 "text-left")

There is no need to send the Authorization field name in Vary because   reuse
of that response for a different user is prohibited by the field   definition ([Section 11.6.2](#section-5.3 "text-left")).   Likewise, if the response content has been selected or influenced by   network region, but the origin server wants the cached response to be   reused even if recipients move from one region to another, then there   is no need for the origin server to indicate such variance in Vary.[¶](#section-5.4 "text-left")


## [13. ](#section-5.5 "text-left")[Conditional Requests](#name-field-values "text-left")       

A conditional request is an HTTP request with one or more request header   fields
that indicate a precondition to be tested before   applying the request method to
the target resource.   [Section 13.2](#section-5.6.1 "text-left") defines when to evaluate preconditions and   their order of precedence when more than one precondition is present.[¶](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")

Conditional GET requests are the most efficient mechanism for HTTP   cache updates
[[CACHING](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")].  Conditionals can also be   applied to
state-changing methods, such as PUT and DELETE, to prevent   the "lost update" problem:
one client accidentally overwriting   the work of another client that has been acting
in parallel.[¶](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")


### [13.1. ](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")[Preconditions](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")         

Preconditions are usually defined with respect to a state of the target   resource
as a whole (its current value set) or the state as observed in a   previously obtained
representation (one value in that set). If a resource   has multiple current representations,
each with its own observable state,   a precondition will assume that the mapping
of each request to a   [selected representation](#section-5.6.3 "text-left") ([Section 3.2](#name-whitespace "text-left"))   is consistent over time.   Regardless, if the mapping is inconsistent or the server is unable to   select an appropriate representation, then no harm will result when the   precondition evaluates to false.[¶](#name-quoted-strings "text-left")

Each precondition defined below consists of a comparison between a   set of validators
obtained from prior representations of the target   resource to the current state
of validators for the selected   representation ([Section 8.8](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")). Hence, these   preconditions evaluate whether the state of the target resource has   changed since a given state known by the client. The effect of such an   evaluation depends on the method semantics and choice of conditional, as   defined in [Section 13.2](#section-5.6.7 "text-left").[¶](#section-5.6.7 "text-left")

Other preconditions, defined by other specifications as extension fields,   might
place conditions on all recipients, on the state of the target   resource in general,
or on a group of resources. For instance, the "If"   header field in WebDAV can make
a request conditional on various aspects   of multiple resources, such as locks,
if the recipient understands and   implements that field ([[WEBDAV](#section-6.1 "text-left")], [Section 10.4](#section-6.1
"text-left")).[¶](#section-6.1 "text-left")

Extensibility of preconditions is only possible when the precondition can   be
safely ignored if unknown (like [If-Modified-Since](#section-6.2 "text-left")), when   deployment can be assumed for a given use case, or when implementation   is signaled by some other property of the target resource. This encourages   a focus on mutually agreed deployment of common standards.[¶](#section-6.3 "text-left")


#### [13.1.1. ](#name-header-fields "text-left")[If-Match](#section-6.4 "text-left")           

The "If-Match" header field makes the request method conditional on the   recipient
origin server either having at least one current   representation of the target resource,
when the field value is "\*", or   having a current representation of the target
resource that has an   entity tag matching a member of the list of entity tags provided
in the   field value.[¶](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")

An origin server MUST use the strong comparison function when comparing   entity
tags for If-Match ([Section 8.8.3.2](#section-6.5 "text-left")), since   the client intends this precondition to prevent the method from being   applied if there have been any changes to the representation data.[¶](#section-6.5.1 "text-left")


```  If-Match = "\*" / #entity-tag```
[¶](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left")
   Examples:[¶](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")

```If-Match: "xyzzy"If-Match: "xyzzy", "r2d2xxxx", "c3piozzzz"If-Match: \*```
[¶](#name-processing-trailer-fields "text-left")
If-Match is most often used with state-changing methods (e.g., POST, PUT,   DELETE)
to prevent accidental overwrites when multiple user agents might be   acting in parallel
on the same resource (i.e., to prevent the "lost update"   problem). In general,
it can be used with any method that involves the   selection or modification of a
representation to abort the request if the   [selected representation](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")'s current entity tag is not a   member within the If-Match field value.[¶](#name-date "text-left")

When an origin server receives a request that selects a representation   and that
request includes an If-Match header field,   the origin server MUST evaluate the
If-Match condition per   [Section 13.2](#section-7 "text-left") prior to performing the method.[¶](#section-7 "text-left")

To evaluate a received If-Match header field:[¶](#name-routing-http-messages "text-left")

1.       If the field value is "\*", the condition is true if the origin server      has a current representation for the target resource.[¶](#name-determining-the-target-reso "text-left")
2.       If the field value is a list of entity tags, the condition is true if      any of the listed tags match the entity tag of the selected representation.[¶](#name-host-and-authority "text-left")
3.       Otherwise, the condition is false.[¶](#section-7.3 "text-left")


An origin server that evaluates an If-Match condition MUST NOT perform   the requested
method if the condition evaluates to false. Instead,   the origin server MAY   indicate
that the conditional request failed by responding with a   [412 (Precondition Failed)](#section-7.3.1 "text-left") status code. Alternatively,   if the request is a state-changing operation that appears to have already   been applied to the selected representation, the origin server
MAY respond   with a [2xx (Successful)](#section-7.3.2 "text-left") status code   (i.e., the change requested by the user agent has already succeeded, but   the user agent might not be aware of it, perhaps because the prior response   was lost or an equivalent change was made by some other user agent).[¶](#name-to-the-origin "text-left")

Allowing an origin server to send a success response when a change request   appears
to have already been applied is more efficient for many authoring   use cases, but
comes with some risk if multiple user agents are making   change requests that are
very similar but not cooperative.   For example, multiple user agents writing to
a common resource as a   semaphore (e.g., a nonatomic increment) are likely to collide
and   potentially lose important state transitions. For those kinds of resources,
an origin server is better off being stringent in sending 412 for every   failed
precondition on an unsafe method.   In other cases, excluding the ETag field from
a success response might   encourage the user agent to perform a GET as its next
request to eliminate   confusion about the resource's current state.[¶](#section-7.6.1 "text-left")

A client MAY send an If-Match header field in a   [GET](#section-7.6.1 "text-left") request to indicate that it would prefer a   [412 (Precondition Failed)](#name-connection "text-left") response if the selected   representation does not match. However, this is only useful in range   requests ([Section 14](#section-7.6.2 "text-left")) for completing a previously   received partial representation when there is no desire for a new   representation. [If-Range](#section-7.6.3 "text-left") ([Section 13.1.5](#section-7.6.3 "text-left"))   is better suited for range requests when the client prefers to receive a   new representation.[¶](#name-via "text-left")

A cache or intermediary MAY ignore If-Match because its   interoperability features
are only necessary for an origin server.[¶](#name-message-transformations "text-left")

Note that an If-Match header field with a list value containing "\*" and   other
values (including other instances of "\*") is syntactically   invalid (therefore
not allowed to be generated) and furthermore is   unlikely to be interoperable.[¶](#name-upgrade "text-left")


#### [13.1.2. ](#section-8 "text-left")[If-None-Match](#name-representation-data-and-met "text-left")           

The "If-None-Match" header field makes the request method conditional on   a recipient
cache or origin server either not having any current   representation of the target
resource, when the field value is "\*", or   having a [selected representation](#name-representation-metadata "text-left") with an entity tag that does not match any   of those listed in the field value.[¶](#section-8.3 "text-left")

A recipient MUST use the weak comparison function when comparing   entity tags
for If-None-Match ([Section 8.8.3.2](#section-8.3.1 "text-left")),   since weak entity tags can be used for cache validation even if there have   been changes to the representation data.[¶](#name-media-type "text-left")


```  If-None-Match = "\*" / #entity-tag```
[¶](#name-charset "text-left")
   Examples:[¶](#section-8.3.3 "text-left")

```If-None-Match: "xyzzy"If-None-Match: W/"xyzzy"If-None-Match: "xyzzy", "r2d2xxxx",
"c3piozzzz"If-None-Match: W/"xyzzy", W/"r2d2xxxx", W/"c3piozzzz"If-None-Match: \*```
[¶](#section-8.4 "text-left")
If-None-Match is primarily used in conditional GET requests to enable   efficient
updates of cached information with a minimum amount of   transaction overhead. When
a client desires to update one or more stored   responses that have entity tags,
the client SHOULD generate an   If-None-Match header field containing a list of those
entity tags when   making a GET request; this allows recipient servers to send a
[304 (Not Modified)](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left") response to indicate when one of those   stored responses matches the selected representation.[¶](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left")

If-None-Match can also be used with a value of "\*" to prevent an unsafe   request
method (e.g., PUT) from inadvertently modifying an existing   representation of the
target resource when the client believes that   the resource does not have a current
representation ([Section 9.2.1](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")).   This is a variation on the "lost update" problem that might arise if more   than one client attempts to create an initial representation for the target   resource.[¶](#section-8.4.1.3 "text-left")

When an origin server receives a request that selects a representation   and that
request includes an If-None-Match header field,   the origin server MUST evaluate
the If-None-Match condition per   [Section 13.2](#section-8.5 "text-left") prior to performing the method.[¶](#section-8.5 "text-left")

To evaluate a received If-None-Match header field:[¶](#section-8.5.1 "text-left")

1.       If the field value is "\*", the condition is false if the origin server      has a current representation for the target resource.[¶](#section-8.6 "text-left")
2.       If the field value is a list of entity tags, the condition is false if      one of the listed tags matches the entity tag of the selected representation.[¶](#section-8.7 "text-left")
3.       Otherwise, the condition is true.[¶](#section-8.7 "text-left")


An origin server that evaluates an If-None-Match condition MUST NOT   perform
the requested method if the condition evaluates to false; instead,   the origin server
MUST respond with either   a) the [304 (Not Modified)](#section-8.8.1 "text-left") status code if the request method   is GET or HEAD or b) the [412 (Precondition Failed)](#section-8.8.1 "text-left") status   code for all other request methods.[¶](#name-weak-versus-strong "text-left")

Requirements on cache handling of a received If-None-Match header field   are
defined in [Section 4.3.2](#section-8.8.2 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#name-last-modified
"text-left")].[¶](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")

Note that an If-None-Match header field with a list value containing "\*" and
other values (including other instances of "\*") is syntactically   invalid (therefore
not allowed to be generated) and furthermore is   unlikely to be interoperable.[¶](#section-8.8.2.2 "text-left")


#### [13.1.3. ](#section-8.8.2.2 "text-left")[If-Modified-Since](#name-comparison "text-left")           

The "If-Modified-Since" header field makes a GET or HEAD request method   conditional
on the [selected representation](#section-8.8.3.1 "text-left")'s modification   date being more   recent than the date provided in the field value. Transfer of the selected   representation's data is avoided if that data has not changed.[¶](#section-8.8.3.2 "text-left")


```  If-Modified-Since = HTTP-date```
[¶](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left")
   An example of the field is:[¶](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left")

```If-Modified-Since: Sat, 29 Oct 1994 19:43:31 GMT```
[¶](#section-9 "text-left")
A recipient MUST ignore If-Modified-Since if the request contains an   [If-None-Match](#section-9 "text-left") header field; the condition in   [If-None-Match](#name-methods "text-left") is considered to be a more accurate   replacement for the condition in If-Modified-Since, and the two are only   combined for the sake of interoperating with older intermediaries that   might not implement [If-None-Match](#name-overview "text-left").[¶](#section-9.2 "text-left")

A recipient MUST ignore the If-Modified-Since header field if the   received field
value is not a valid HTTP-date, the field value has more than   one member, or if
the request method is neither GET nor HEAD.[¶](#section-9.2.1 "text-left")

A recipient MUST ignore the If-Modified-Since header field if the   resource does
not have a modification date available.[¶](#section-9.2.2 "text-left")

A recipient MUST interpret an If-Modified-Since field value's timestamp   in terms
of the origin server's clock.[¶](#name-idempotent-methods "text-left")

If-Modified-Since is typically used for two distinct purposes:   1) to allow efficient
updates of a cached representation that does not   have an entity tag and 2) to limit
the scope of a web traversal to resources   that have recently changed.[¶](#section-9.3 "text-left")

When used for cache updates, a cache will typically use the value of the   cached
message's [Last-Modified](#section-9.3 "text-left") header field to generate the field   value of If-Modified-Since. This behavior is most interoperable for cases   where clocks are poorly synchronized or when the server has chosen to only   honor exact timestamp matches (due to a problem with Last-Modified dates   that appear to go "back in time" when the origin server's clock is   corrected or a representation is restored from an archived backup).   However, caches occasionally generate the field value based on other data,   such as the [Date](#section-9.3.2 "text-left") header field of the cached message or the   clock time at which the message was received, particularly when the   cached message does not contain a [Last-Modified](#section-9.3.3 "text-left") header field.[¶](#section-9.3.3 "text-left")

When used for limiting the scope of retrieval to a recent time window, a   user
agent will generate an If-Modified-Since field value based on either   its own clock
or a [Date](#section-9.3.4 "text-left") header field received from the   server in a prior response. Origin servers that choose an exact   timestamp match based on the selected representation's   [Last-Modified](#section-9.3.5 "text-left")   header field will not be able to help the user agent limit its data   transfers to only those changed during the specified window.[¶](#section-9.3.6 "text-left")

When an origin server receives a request that selects a representation   and that
request includes an If-Modified-Since header field without an   [If-None-Match](#section-9.3.7 "text-left") header field, the origin server
SHOULD   evaluate the If-Modified-Since condition per   [Section 13.2](#section-9.3.7 "text-left") prior to performing the method.[¶](#section-9.3.8 "text-left")

To evaluate a received If-Modified-Since header field:[¶](#section-9.3.8 "text-left")

1.       If the selected representation's last modification date is earlier or      equal to the date provided in the field value, the condition is false.[¶](#name-message-context "text-left")
2.       Otherwise, the condition is true.[¶](#section-10.1 "text-left")


An origin server that evaluates an If-Modified-Since condition   SHOULD NOT perform
the requested method if the condition evaluates to   false; instead,   the origin
server SHOULD generate a [304 (Not Modified)](rfc9110.xml "Roy T. Fielding")   response, including only those metadata that are useful for identifying or   updating a previously cached response.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

Requirements on cache handling of a received If-Modified-Since header field   
are defined in [Section 4.3.2](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc9110.xml
"rfc-local.css")].[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")


#### [13.1.4. ](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")[If-Unmodified-Since](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")           

The "If-Unmodified-Since" header field makes the request method conditional   
on the [selected representation](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")'s last modification date being   earlier than or equal to the date provided in the field value.   This field accomplishes the   same purpose as [If-Match](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") for cases where the user agent does   not have an entity tag for the representation.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")


```  If-Unmodified-Since = HTTP-date```
[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
   An example of the field is:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

```If-Unmodified-Since: Sat, 29 Oct 1994 19:43:31 GMT```
[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
A recipient MUST ignore If-Unmodified-Since if the request contains an   [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field; the condition in   [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") is considered to be a more accurate replacement for   the condition in If-Unmodified-Since, and the two are only combined for the   sake of interoperating with older intermediaries that might not implement   [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

A recipient MUST ignore the If-Unmodified-Since header field if the   received
field value is not a valid HTTP-date (including when the field   value appears to
be a list of dates).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

A recipient MUST ignore the If-Unmodified-Since header field if the   resource
does not have a modification date available.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

A recipient MUST interpret an If-Unmodified-Since field value's timestamp   in
terms of the origin server's clock.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

If-Unmodified-Since is most often used with state-changing methods   (e.g., POST,
PUT, DELETE) to prevent accidental overwrites when multiple   user agents might be
acting in parallel on a resource that does   not supply entity tags with its representations
(i.e., to prevent the   "lost update" problem).   In general, it can be used with
any method that involves the selection   or modification of a representation to abort
the request if the   [selected representation](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")'s last modification date has   changed since the date provided in the If-Unmodified-Since field value.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

When an origin server receives a request that selects a representation   and that
request includes an If-Unmodified-Since header field without   an [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field,   the origin server
MUST evaluate the If-Unmodified-Since condition per   [Section 13.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") prior to performing the method.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

To evaluate a received If-Unmodified-Since header field:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

1.       If the selected representation's last modification date is earlier than or      equal to the date provided in the field value, the condition is true.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
2.       Otherwise, the condition is false.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")


An origin server that evaluates an If-Unmodified-Since condition MUST NOT   perform
the requested method if the condition evaluates to false.   Instead, the origin server
MAY indicate that the conditional request   failed by responding with a [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   status code. Alternatively, if the request is a state-changing operation   that appears to have already been applied to the selected representation,   the origin server
MAY respond with a [2xx (Successful)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   status code   (i.e., the change requested by the user agent has already succeeded, but   the user agent might not be aware of it, perhaps because the prior response   was lost or an equivalent change was made by some other user agent).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

Allowing an origin server to send a success response when a change request   appears
to have already been applied is more efficient for many authoring   use cases, but
comes with some risk if multiple user agents are making   change requests that are
very similar but not cooperative.   In those cases, an origin server is better off
being stringent in sending   412 for every failed precondition on an unsafe method.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

A client MAY send an If-Unmodified-Since header field in a   [GET](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") request to indicate that it would prefer a   [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") response if the selected   representation has been modified. However, this is only useful in range   requests ([Section 14](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) for completing a previously   received partial representation when there is no desire for a new   representation. [If-Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") ([Section 13.1.5](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"))   is better suited for range requests when the client prefers to receive a   new representation.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

A cache or intermediary MAY ignore If-Unmodified-Since because its   interoperability
features are only necessary for an origin server.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")


#### [13.1.5. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[If-Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           

The "If-Range" header field provides a special conditional request   mechanism
that is similar to the [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") and   [If-Unmodified-Since](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header fields but that instructs the   recipient to ignore the [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field if the validator   doesn't match, resulting in transfer of the new [selected representation](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   instead of a [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

If a client has a partial copy of a representation and wishes   to have an up-to-date
copy of the entire representation, it could use the   [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field with a conditional GET (using   either or both of [If-Unmodified-Since](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") and   [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").) However, if the precondition fails because the   representation has been modified, the client would then have to make a   second request to obtain the entire current representation.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

The "If-Range" header field allows a client to "short-circuit" the second   request.
Informally, its meaning is as follows: if the representation is unchanged,   send
me the part(s) that I am requesting in Range; otherwise, send me the   entire representation.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")


```  If-Range = entity-tag / HTTP-date```
[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
   A valid [entity-tag](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") can be distinguished from a valid   [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") by examining the first three characters for a   DQUOTE.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A client MUST NOT generate an If-Range header field in a request that   does not 
contain a [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field.   A server MUST ignore 
an If-Range header field received in a request that   does not contain a [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field.   An origin server
MUST ignore an If-Range header field received in a   request for a target resource 
that does not support Range requests.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A client MUST NOT generate an If-Range header field containing an   entity tag 
that is marked as weak.   A client MUST NOT generate an If-Range header field containing 
an   [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") unless the client has no entity tag for   the corresponding representation and the date is a strong validator   in the sense defined by [Section 8.8.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A server that receives an If-Range header field on a Range request MUST   evaluate 
the condition per [Section 13.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") prior to   performing the method.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   To evaluate a received If-Range header field containing an   [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"):[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

                  1. If the [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") validator provided is not a       strong validator in the sense defined by       [Section 8.8.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), the condition is false.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            2. If the [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") validator provided exactly matches       the [Last-Modified](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") field value for the selected       representation, the condition is true.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            3. Otherwise, the condition is false.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

   To evaluate a received If-Range header field containing an   [entity-tag](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"):[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

                  1. If the [entity-tag](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") validator provided exactly matches       the [ETag](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") field value for the selected representation       using the strong comparison function       ([Section 8.8.3.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")), the condition is true.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            2. Otherwise, the condition is false.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

   A recipient of an If-Range header field MUST ignore the   [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field if the If-Range condition   evaluates to false. Otherwise, the recipient
SHOULD process the   [Range](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field as requested.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Note that the If-Range comparison is by exact match, including when the   validator 
is an [HTTP-date](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), and so it   differs from the "earlier than or equal to" comparison used when evaluating   an [If-Unmodified-Since](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") conditional.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [13.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Evaluation of Preconditions](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        

          
#### [13.2.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[When to Evaluate](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   Except when excluded below, a recipient cache or origin server MUST   evaluate 
received request preconditions after it has successfully performed   its normal request 
checks and just before it would process the request content   (if any) or perform 
the action associated with the request method.   A server MUST ignore all received 
preconditions if its response to the   same request without those conditions, prior 
to processing the request content,   would have been a status code other than a [2xx (Successful)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   or [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").   In other words, redirects and failures that can be detected before   significant processing occurs take precedence over the evaluation   of preconditions.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A server that is not the origin server for the target resource and cannot   act 
as a cache for requests on the target resource MUST NOT evaluate the   conditional 
request header fields defined by this specification, and it   MUST forward them if 
the request is forwarded, since the generating   client intends that they be evaluated 
by a server that can provide a   current representation.   Likewise, a server MUST 
ignore the conditional request header fields   defined by this specification when 
received with a request method that does   not involve the selection or modification 
of a   [selected representation](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), such as CONNECT, OPTIONS, or TRACE.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Note that protocol extensions can modify the conditions under which   preconditions 
are evaluated or the consequences of their evaluation.   For example, the immutable 
cache directive   (defined by [[RFC8246](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")]) instructs 
caches to forgo   forwarding conditional requests when they hold a fresh response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Although conditional request header fields are defined as being usable with   
the HEAD method (to keep HEAD's semantics consistent with those of GET),   there 
is no point in sending a conditional HEAD because a successful   response is around 
the same size as a [304 (Not Modified)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   response and more useful than a [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [13.2.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Precedence of Preconditions](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   When more than one conditional request header field is present in a request,  
 the order in which the fields are evaluated becomes important. In practice,   the 
fields defined in this document are consistently implemented in a   single, logical 
order, since "lost update" preconditions have more strict   requirements than cache 
validation, a validated cache is more efficient   than a partial response, and entity 
tags are presumed to be more accurate   than date validators.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A recipient cache or origin server MUST evaluate the request   preconditions defined 
by this specification in the following order:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

                  1. 

              When recipient is the origin server and     [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") is present,     evaluate the [If-Match](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") precondition:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
2. if true, continue to step [3](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                3. if false, respond [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") unless           it can be determined that the state-changing request has already           succeeded (see [Section 13.1.1](rfc-local.css "section-abstract"))[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
              
            
4. 

              
When recipient is the origin server,     [If-Match](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is not present, and     [If-Unmodified-Since](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is present,     evaluate the [If-Unmodified-Since](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") precondition:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

5. if true, continue to step [3](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                6. if false, respond [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") unless           it can be determined that the state-changing request has already           succeeded (see [Section 13.1.4](rfc-local.css "section-abstract"))[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
              
            
7. 

              
When [If-None-Match](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is present,     evaluate the [If-None-Match](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") precondition:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

8. if true, continue to step [5](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                9. if false for GET/HEAD, respond [304 (Not Modified)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                10. if false for other methods, respond [412 (Precondition Failed)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
              
            
11. 

              
When the method is GET or HEAD,     [If-None-Match](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is not present, and     [If-Modified-Since](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is present,     evaluate the [If-Modified-Since](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") precondition:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

12. if true, continue to step [5](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                13. if false, respond [304 (Not Modified)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
              
            
14. 

              
When the method is GET and both     [Range](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") and [If-Range](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") are present,     evaluate the [If-Range](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") precondition:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

15. if true and the [Range](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") is           applicable to the [selected representation](rfc-local.css "section-abstract"),           respond [206 (Partial Content)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
                16. otherwise, ignore the [Range](urn:issn:2070-1721 "section-abstract") header field           and respond [200 (OK)](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")
              
            
17. 

              
Otherwise,[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

18. perform the requested method and           respond according to its success or failure.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")
              
          


   Any extension to HTTP that defines additional conditional request   header fields 
ought to define the order   for evaluating such fields in relation to those defined 
in this document   and other conditionals that might be found in practice.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2818 "section-abstract")

      
## [14. ](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7231 "section-abstract")[Range Requests](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7232 "section-abstract")      
   Clients often encounter interrupted data   transfers as a result of canceled requests 
or dropped connections. When a   client has stored a partial representation, it is 
desirable to request the   remainder of that representation in a subsequent request 
rather than   transfer the entire representation. Likewise, devices with limited 
local   storage might benefit from being able to request only a subset of a larger 
  representation, such as a single page of a very large document, or the   dimensions 
of an embedded image.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

   Range requests are an OPTIONAL feature   of HTTP, designed so that recipients 
not implementing this feature (or not   supporting it for the target resource) can 
respond as if it is a normal   GET request without impacting interoperability. Partial 
responses are   indicated by a distinct status code to not be mistaken for full responses 
  by caches that might not implement the feature.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

        
### [14.1. ](#abstract "section-abstract")[Range Units](#abstract "section-abstract")        
   Representation data can be partitioned into subranges when there are   addressable 
structural units inherent to that data's content coding or   media type. For example, 
octet (a.k.a. byte) boundaries are a structural   unit common to all representation 
data, allowing partitions of the data to   be identified as a range of bytes at some 
offset from the start or end of   that data.[¶](#section-abstract-1 "text-left")

   This general notion of a "range unit" is used   in the [Accept-Ranges](#section-abstract-1 "text-left") ([Section 14.3](#section-abstract-1 "text-left"))   response header field to advertise support for range requests, the   [Range](#section-abstract-2 "text-left") ([Section 14.2](#section-abstract-2 "text-left")) request header field   to delineate the parts of a representation that are requested, and the   [Content-Range](#name-status-of-this-memo "text-left") ([Section 14.4](#name-status-of-this-memo "text-left"))   header field to describe which part of a representation is being   transferred.[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-1 "text-left")
        

```  range-unit       = token```
[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")
All range unit names are case-insensitive and ought to be registered   within
the "HTTP Range Unit Registry", as defined in   [Section 16.5.1](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left").[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")

Range units are intended to be extensible, as described in   [Section 16.5](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110 "text-left").[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110 "text-left")


#### [14.1.1. ](#section-boilerplate.1-3 "text-left")[Range Specifiers](#name-copyright-notice "text-left")           

Ranges are expressed in terms of a range unit paired with a set of range   specifiers.
The range unit name determines what kinds of range-spec   are applicable to its own
specifiers. Hence, the following grammar is   generic: each range unit is expected
to specify requirements on when   [int-range](https://trustee.ietf.org/license-info "text-left"), [suffix-range](https://trustee.ietf.org/license-info "text-left"), and   [other-range](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left") are allowed.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")


A range request can specify a single range or a set   of ranges within a
single representation.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")


```  ranges-specifier = range-unit "=" range-set  range-set        = 1#range-spec 
 range-spec       = int-range                   / suffix-range                   
/ other-range```
[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")


         An [int-range](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left") is a range expressed as two non-negative   integers or as one non-negative integer through to the end of the   representation data.   The range unit specifies what the integers mean (e.g., they might indicate   unit offsets from the beginning, inclusive numbered parts, etc.).[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")
                              

```  int-range     = first-pos "-" [ last-pos ]  first-pos     = 1\*DIGIT  last-pos
= 1\*DIGIT```
[¶](#section-1 "text-left")
An [int-range](#section-1 "text-left") is invalid if the   [last-pos](#section-1 "text-left") value is present and less than the   [first-pos](#name-introduction "text-left").[¶](#section-1.1 "text-left")


A [suffix-range](#section-1.1 "text-left") is a range expressed as a suffix of the   representation data with the provided non-negative integer maximum length   (in range units). In other words, the last N units of the representation   data.[¶](#section-1.2 "text-left")


```  suffix-range  = "-" suffix-length  suffix-length = 1\*DIGIT```
[¶](#section-1.3 "text-left")


     To provide for extensibility, the [other-range](#section-1.4 "text-left") rule is a   mostly unconstrained grammar that allows application-specific or future   range units to define additional range specifiers.[¶](#name-specifications-obsoleted-by "text-left")
          

```  other-range   = 1\*( %x21-2B / %x2D-7E )                ; 1\*(VCHAR excluding
comma)```
[¶](#name-conformance "text-left")
A [ranges-specifier](#section-2.1 "text-left") is invalid if it contains any   [range-spec](#section-2.1 "text-left") that is invalid or undefined for the indicated   [range-unit](#section-2.2 "text-left").[¶](#section-2.2 "text-left")


A valid [ranges-specifier](#section-2.2 "text-left") is "satisfiable"   if it contains at least one [range-spec](#section-2.3 "text-left") that is   satisfiable, as defined by the indicated [range-unit](#section-2.3 "text-left").   Otherwise, the [ranges-specifier](#name-length-requirements "text-left") is   "unsatisfiable".[¶](#section-2.4 "text-left")


#### [14.1.2. ](#section-2.4 "text-left")[Byte Ranges](#section-2.5 "text-left")          
The "bytes" range unit is used to express subranges of a representation   data's
octet sequence.   Each byte range is expressed as an integer range at some offset,
relative   to either the beginning ([int-range](#section-3 "text-left")) or end   ([suffix-range](#section-3 "text-left")) of the representation data.   Byte ranges do not use the [other-range](#name-terminology-and-core-concep "text-left") specifier.[¶](#section-3.1 "text-left")

The [first-pos](#section-3.1 "text-left") value in a bytes [int-range](#section-3.1 "text-left")   gives the offset of the first byte in a range.   The [last-pos](#section-3.2 "text-left") value gives the offset of the last   byte in the range; that is, the byte positions specified are inclusive.   Byte offsets start at zero.[¶](#name-representations "text-left")

If the representation data has a content coding applied, each byte range is   
calculated with respect to the encoded sequence of bytes, not the sequence   of underlying
bytes that would be obtained after decoding.[¶](#section-3.4 "text-left")

Examples of bytes range specifiers:[¶](#section-3.4 "text-left")

-               The first 500 bytes (byte offsets 0-499, inclusive):[¶](#section-3.5 "text-left")		   bytes=0-499[¶](#name-user-agents "text-left")
-               The second 500 bytes (byte offsets 500-999, inclusive):[¶](#section-3.6 "text-left")		   bytes=500-999[¶](#section-3.7 "text-left")


A client can limit the number of bytes requested without knowing the size   of
the [selected representation](#section-3.8 "text-left").   If the [last-pos](#section-3.8 "text-left") value is absent, or if the value is   greater than or equal to the current length of the representation data, the   byte range is interpreted as the remainder of the representation (i.e., the   server replaces the value of [last-pos](#section-3.9 "text-left") with a value that   is one less than the current length of the selected representation).[¶](#section-4 "text-left")

A client can refer to the last N bytes (N > 0) of the selected   representation
using a [suffix-range](#section-4 "text-left").   If the selected representation is shorter than the specified   [suffix-length](#section-4.1 "text-left"), the entire representation is used.[¶](#section-4.1 "text-left")

Additional examples, assuming a representation of length 10000:[¶](#section-4.2 "text-left")

-               The final 500 bytes (byte offsets 9500-9999, inclusive):[¶](#name-http-related-uri-schemes "text-left")		   bytes=-500[¶](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")Or:[¶](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")		   bytes=9500-[¶](#section-4.2.2 "text-left")
-               The first and last bytes only (bytes 0 and 9999):[¶](#name-https-uri-scheme "text-left")		   bytes=0-0,-1[¶](#section-4.2.3 "text-left")
-               The first, middle, and last 1000 bytes:[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")		   bytes= 0-999, 4500-5499, -1000[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")
-               Other valid (but not canonical) specifications of the second 500      bytes (byte offsets 500-999, inclusive):[¶](#section-4.2.5 "text-left")		   bytes=500-600,601-999     bytes=500-700,601-999[¶](#section-4.3 "text-left")


For a [GET](#section-4.3 "text-left") request, a valid bytes [range-spec](#section-4.3 "text-left")   is [satisfiable](#name-authoritative-access "text-left") if it is either:[¶](#section-4.3.1 "text-left")

- an [int-range](#section-4.3.1 "text-left") with a [first-pos](#section-4.3.1 "text-left") that       is less than the current length of the selected representation        or[¶](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")
- a [suffix-range](#section-4.3.2 "text-left") with a non-zero       [suffix-length](#section-4.3.3 "text-left").[¶](#section-4.3.3 "text-left")


When a selected representation has zero length, the only   [satisfiable](#name-https-origins "text-left") form of [range-spec](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") in a   [GET](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") request is a [suffix-range](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") with a   non-zero [suffix-length](#section-4.3.4 "text-left").[¶](#name-https-certificate-verificat "text-left")

In the byte-range syntax, [first-pos](#section-4.3.5 "text-left"),   [last-pos](#section-4.3.5 "text-left"), and [suffix-length](#section-4.3.5 "text-left") are   expressed as decimal number of octets. Since there is no predefined limit   to the length of content, recipients
MUST anticipate potentially   large decimal numerals and prevent parsing errors
due to integer conversion   overflows.[¶](#section-5 "text-left")


### [14.2. ](#section-5.1 "text-left")[Range](#section-5.1 "text-left")          

The "Range" header field on a GET request modifies the method semantics to   request
transfer of only one or more subranges of the   selected representation data ([Section 8.1](#section-5.3 "text-left")),   rather than the entire [selected representation](#section-5.3 "text-left").[¶](#name-field-order "text-left")


```  Range = ranges-specifier```
[¶](#name-field-limits "text-left")
   A server MAY ignore the Range header field. However, origin servers and   intermediate 
caches ought to support byte ranges when possible, since they   support efficient 
recovery from partially failed transfers and partial   retrieval of large representations.[¶](#section-5.6 "text-left")

   A server MUST ignore a Range header field received with a request method   that 
is unrecognized or for which range handling is not defined. For this   specification, 
[GET](#section-5.6.1 "text-left") is the only method for which range handling   is defined.[¶](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")

   An origin server MUST ignore a Range header field that contains a range   unit 
it does not understand. A proxy MAY discard a Range header   field that contains 
a range unit it does not understand.[¶](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")

   A server that supports range requests MAY ignore or reject a   [Range](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left") header field that contains an invalid   [ranges-specifier](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left") ([Section 14.1.1](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")),   a [ranges-specifier](#name-recipient-requirements "text-left") with more than two overlapping ranges,   or a set of many small ranges that are not listed in ascending order,   since these are indications of either a broken client or a deliberate   denial-of-service attack ([Section 17.15](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")).   A client
SHOULD NOT request multiple ranges that are inherently less   efficient to process 
and transfer than a single range that encompasses the   same data.[¶](#section-5.6.3 "text-left")

   A server that supports range requests MAY ignore a [Range](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")   header field when the selected representation has no content   (i.e., the selected representation's data is of zero length).[¶](#name-quoted-strings "text-left")

   A client that is requesting multiple ranges SHOULD list those ranges in   ascending 
order (the order in which they would typically be received in a   complete representation) 
unless there is a specific need to request a later   part earlier. For example, a 
user agent processing a large representation   with an internal catalog of parts 
might need to request later parts first,   particularly if the representation consists 
of pages stored in reverse   order and the user agent wishes to transfer one page 
at a time.[¶](#section-5.6.7 "text-left")

   The Range header field is evaluated after evaluating the precondition header  
 fields defined in [Section 13.1](#name-date-time-formats "text-left"), and only if the result in absence   of the Range header field would be a [200 (OK)](#section-6 "text-left") response. In   other words, Range is ignored when a conditional GET would result in a   [304 (Not Modified)](#section-6 "text-left") response.[¶](#name-message-abstraction "text-left")

   The If-Range header field ([Section 13.1.5](#section-6.1 "text-left")) can be used as   a precondition to applying the Range header field.[¶](#section-6.1 "text-left")

   If all of the preconditions are true, the server supports the Range header   field 
for the target resource, the received Range field-value contains a   valid [ranges-specifier](#section-6.2 "text-left") with a [range-unit](#name-control-data "text-left")   supported for that target resource, and that   [ranges-specifier](#section-6.3 "text-left") is [satisfiable](#section-6.3 "text-left") with respect   to the selected representation,   the server
SHOULD send a [206 (Partial Content)](#section-6.4 "text-left") response   with content containing one or more partial representations   that correspond to the satisfiable [range-spec](#name-content "text-left")(s) requested.[¶](#section-6.4.1 "text-left")

   The above does not imply that a server will send all requested ranges.   In some 
cases, it may only be possible (or efficient) to send a portion of   the requested 
ranges first, while expecting the client to re-request the   remaining portions later 
if they are still desired   (see [Section 15.3.7](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")).[¶](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")

   If all of the preconditions are true, the server supports the Range header   field 
for the target resource, the received Range field-value contains a   valid [ranges-specifier](#section-6.5 "text-left"), and either the   [range-unit](#section-6.5 "text-left") is not supported for that target resource or   the [ranges-specifier](#section-6.5.1 "text-left") is unsatisfiable with respect to   the selected representation, the server
SHOULD send a   [416 (Range Not Satisfiable)](#section-6.5.1 "text-left") response.[¶](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left")

        
### [14.3. ](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")[Accept-Ranges](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")         
                       
   The "Accept-Ranges" field in a response indicates whether an upstream   server 
supports range requests for the target resource.[¶](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")
                

```  Accept-Ranges     = acceptable-ranges  acceptable-ranges = 1#range-unit```
[¶](#section-6.6.2 "text-left")
For example, a server that supports   [byte-range requests](#name-trailer "text-left") ([Section 14.1.2](#section-7 "text-left"))
can send the field[¶](#section-7 "text-left")

```Accept-Ranges: bytes```
[¶](#section-7.1 "text-left")
   to indicate that it supports byte range requests for that target resource,   thereby 
encouraging its use by the client for future partial requests on   the same request 
path.   Range units are defined in [Section 14.1](#name-determining-the-target-reso "text-left").[¶](#section-7.2 "text-left")

   A client MAY generate range requests regardless of having received an   Accept-Ranges 
field. The information only provides advice for the sake of   improving performance 
and reducing unnecessary network transfers.[¶](#section-7.3 "text-left")

   Conversely, a client MUST NOT assume that receiving an Accept-Ranges field   means 
that future range requests will return partial responses. The content might   change, 
the server might only support range requests at certain times or under   certain 
conditions, or a different intermediary might process the next request.[¶](#section-7.3.1 "text-left")

   A server that does not support any kind of range request for the target   resource 
MAY send[¶](#section-7.3.2 "text-left")

```Accept-Ranges: none```
[¶](#section-7.3.3 "text-left")
to advise the client not to attempt a range request on the same request path.
The range unit "none" is reserved for this purpose.[¶](#name-to-the-origin "text-left")

The Accept-Ranges field MAY be sent in a trailer section, but is preferred   to
be sent as a header field because the information is particularly useful   for restarting
large information transfers that have failed in mid-content   (before the trailer
section is received).[¶](#section-7.5 "text-left")


### [14.4. ](#section-7.5 "text-left")[Content-Range](#section-7.6 "text-left")         

The "Content-Range" header field is sent in a single part   [206 (Partial Content)](#section-7.6.1 "text-left") response to indicate the partial range   of the [selected representation](#section-7.6.1 "text-left") enclosed as the message content, sent in   each part of a multipart 206 response to indicate the range enclosed within   each body part ([Section 14.6](#section-7.6.2 "text-left")), and sent in [416 (Range Not Satisfiable)](#name-max-forwards "text-left")   responses to provide information about the selected representation.[¶](#section-7.6.3 "text-left")


```  Content-Range       = range-unit SP                        ( range-resp / unsatisfied-range 
)  range-resp          = incl-range "/" ( complete-length / "\*" )  incl-range   
       = first-pos "-" last-pos  unsatisfied-range   = "\*/" complete-length  complete-length 
    = 1\*DIGIT```
[¶](#section-8 "text-left")
   If a [206 (Partial Content)](#section-8.1 "text-left") response contains a   [Content-Range](#section-8.1 "text-left") header field with a [range unit](#section-8.1 "text-left")   ([Section 14.1](#section-8.1 "text-left")) that the recipient does not understand, the   recipient
MUST NOT attempt to recombine it with a stored representation.   A proxy that receives 
such a message SHOULD forward it downstream.[¶](#name-representation-metadata "text-left")

   Content-Range might also be sent as a request modifier to request a   partial 
PUT, as described in [Section 14.5](#section-8.3 "text-left"), based on private   agreements between client and origin server.   A server
MUST ignore a Content-Range header field received in a request   with a method for 
which Content-Range support is not defined.[¶](#section-8.3.1 "text-left")

   For byte ranges, a sender SHOULD indicate the complete length of the   representation 
from which the range has been extracted, unless the complete   length is unknown 
or difficult to determine. An asterisk character ("\*") in   place of the complete-length 
indicates that the representation length was   unknown when the header field was 
generated.[¶](#section-8.3.3 "text-left")

   The following example illustrates when the complete length of the selected   representation 
is known by the sender to be 1234 bytes:[¶](#name-multipart-types "text-left")

```Content-Range: bytes 42-1233/1234```
[¶](#section-8.4 "text-left")
and this second example illustrates when the complete length is unknown:[¶](#section-8.4.1 "text-left")

```Content-Range: bytes 42-1233/\*```
[¶](#section-8.4.1 "text-left")
   A Content-Range field value is invalid if it contains a   [range-resp](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left") that has a [last-pos](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left")   value less than its [first-pos](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left") value, or a   [complete-length](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left") value less than or equal to its   [last-pos](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left") value. The recipient of an invalid   [Content-Range](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")         
MUST NOT attempt to recombine the received   content with a stored representation.[¶](#name-deflate-coding "text-left")

   A server generating a [416 (Range Not Satisfiable)](#section-8.4.1.3 "text-left") response   to a byte-range request
SHOULD send a Content-Range header field with an   [unsatisfied-range](#name-gzip-coding "text-left") value, as in the following example:[¶](#section-8.5 "text-left")

```Content-Range: bytes \*/1234```
[¶](#section-8.5 "text-left")
The complete-length in a 416 response indicates the current length of the   selected
representation.[¶](#section-8.5.1 "text-left")

The Content-Range header field has no meaning for status codes that do   not explicitly
describe its semantic. For this specification, only the   [206 (Partial Content)](#section-8.6 "text-left") and   [416 (Range Not Satisfiable)](#section-8.6 "text-left") status codes describe a meaning   for Content-Range.[¶](#name-content-length "text-left")

The following are examples of Content-Range values in which the   selected representation
contains a total of 1234 bytes:[¶](#section-8.7 "text-left")

-             The first 500 bytes:[¶](#section-8.8 "text-left")		Content-Range: bytes 0-499/1234[¶](#name-validator-fields "text-left")
-             The second 500 bytes:[¶](#section-8.8.1 "text-left")		Content-Range: bytes 500-999/1234[¶](#section-8.8.2 "text-left")
-             All except for the first 500 bytes:[¶](#name-last-modified "text-left")		Content-Range: bytes 500-1233/1234[¶](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")
-             The last 500 bytes:[¶](#name-generation "text-left")		Content-Range: bytes 734-1233/1234[¶](#section-8.8.2.2 "text-left")


### [14.5. ](#section-8.8.3 "text-left")[Partial PUT](#section-8.8.3 "text-left")         

Some origin servers support [PUT](#section-8.8.3.1 "text-left") of a partial representation   when the user agent sends a [Content-Range](#name-generation-2 "text-left") header field   ([Section 14.4](#section-8.8.3.2 "text-left")) in the request, though   such support is inconsistent and depends on private agreements with   user agents. In general, it requests that the state of the   [target resource](#name-comparison-2 "text-left") be partly replaced with the enclosed content   at an offset and length indicated by the Content-Range value, where the   offset is relative to the current selected representation.[¶](#name-example-entity-tags-varying "text-left")

An origin server SHOULD respond with a [400 (Bad Request)](#section-9 "text-left")   status code if it receives [Content-Range](#section-9 "text-left") on a PUT for a   target resource that does not support partial PUT requests.[¶](#section-9 "text-left")

Partial PUT is not backwards compatible with the original definition of PUT.  
It may result in the content being written as a complete replacement for the   current
representation.[¶](#section-9.1 "text-left")

Partial resource updates are also possible by targeting a separately   identified
resource with state that overlaps or extends a portion of the   larger resource,
or by using a different method that has been specifically   defined for partial updates
(for example, the PATCH method defined in   [[RFC5789](#section-9.2.1 "text-left")]).[¶](#section-9.2.1 "text-left")


### [14.6. ](#name-safe-methods "text-left")[Media Type multipart/byteranges](#section-9.2.2 "text-left")         

When a [206 (Partial Content)](#section-9.2.3 "text-left") response message includes the   content of multiple ranges, they are transmitted as body parts in a   multipart message body
([[RFC2046](#name-methods-and-caching "text-left")], [Section 5.1](#section-9.3 "text-left"))
with the media type of "multipart/byteranges".[¶](#section-9.3 "text-left")

The "multipart/byteranges" media type includes one or more body parts, each   
with its own [Content-Type](#section-9.3.1 "text-left") and [Content-Range](#section-9.3.1 "text-left")   fields. The required boundary parameter specifies the boundary string used   to separate each body part.[¶](#section-9.3.2 "text-left")

Implementation Notes:[¶](#section-9.3.2 "text-left")

1. Additional CRLFs might precede the first boundary string in the body.[¶](#section-9.3.3 "text-left")
2. Although [[RFC2046](#section-9.3.3 "text-left")] permits the boundary string to be      quoted, some existing implementations handle a quoted boundary      string incorrectly.[¶](#section-9.3.4 "text-left")
3. A number of clients and servers were coded to an early draft      of the byteranges specification that used a media type of      "multipart/x-byteranges",      which is almost (but not quite) compatible with this type.[¶](#section-9.3.6 "text-left")


Despite the name, the "multipart/byteranges" media type is not limited to   byte
ranges. The following example uses an "exampleunit" range unit:[¶](#section-9.3.7 "text-left")

```HTTP/1.1 206 Partial ContentDate: Tue, 14 Nov 1995 06:25:24 GMTLast-Modified: 
Tue, 14 July 04:58:08 GMTContent-Length: 2331785Content-Type: multipart/byteranges; 
boundary=THIS_STRING_SEPARATES--THIS_STRING_SEPARATESContent-Type: video/exampleContent-Range: 
exampleunit 1.2-4.3/25...the first range...--THIS_STRING_SEPARATESContent-Type:
video/exampleContent-Range: exampleunit 11.2-14.3/25...the second range--THIS_STRING_SEPARATES--```
[¶](#section-10 "text-left")
  The following information serves as the registration form for the  "multipart/byteranges" 
media type.[¶](#name-message-context "text-left")
          Type name:          multipart[¶](#section-10.1 "text-left")          Subtype 
name:          byteranges[¶](rfc9110.xml "Roy T. Fielding")          Required parameters: 
         boundary[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")          Optional parameters: 
         N/A[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")          Encoding considerations:  
        only "7bit", "8bit", or "binary" are permitted[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") 
         Security considerations:          see [Section 17](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") 
         Interoperability considerations:          N/A[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") 
         Published specification:          RFC 9110 (see [Section 14.6](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css"))[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") 
         Applications that use this media type:          HTTP components supporting 
multiple ranges in a single request[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          Fragment 
identifier considerations:          N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")       
   Additional information:                                    Deprecated alias names 
for this type:              N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")              Magic 
number(s):              N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")              File 
extension(s):              N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")              Macintosh 
file type code(s):              N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
           Person and email address to contact for further information:          
See Authors' Addresses section.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          Intended 
usage:          COMMON[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          Restrictions on 
usage:          N/A[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          Author:          
See Authors' Addresses section.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          Change 
controller:          IESG[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        

      
## [15. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Status Codes](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")       
     
   The status code of a response is a three-digit integer code that describes   the 
result of the request and the semantics of the response, including   whether the 
request was successful and what content is enclosed (if any).   All valid status 
codes are within the range of 100 to 599, inclusive.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   The first digit of the status code defines the class of response. The   last two 
digits do not have any categorization role. There are five   values for the first 
digit:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

-           [1xx (Informational)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"): The request was received, continuing    process[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
        -           [2xx (Successful)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"): The request was successfully received,    understood, and accepted[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
        -           [3xx (Redirection)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"): Further action needs to be taken in order to    complete the request[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
        -           [4xx (Client Error)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"): The request contains bad syntax or cannot    be fulfilled[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
        -           [5xx (Server Error)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"): The server failed to fulfill an apparently    valid request[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
      

   HTTP status codes are extensible. A client is not required to understand   the 
meaning of all registered status codes, though such understanding is   obviously 
desirable. However, a client MUST understand the class of any   status code, as indicated 
by the first digit, and treat an unrecognized   status code as being equivalent to 
the x00 status code of that class.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   For example, if a client receives an unrecognized status code of 471,   it can 
see from the first digit that there was something wrong with its   request and treat 
the response as if it had received a   [400 (Bad Request)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") status code. The response   message will usually contain a representation that explains the status.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Values outside the range 100..599 are invalid. Implementations often use   three-digit 
integer values outside of that range (i.e., 600..999) for   internal communication 
of non-HTTP status (e.g., library errors). A client   that receives a response with 
an invalid status code SHOULD process the   response as if it had a [5xx (Server Error)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") status code.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")


                              A single request can have multiple associated responses: 
zero or more  "interim" (non-final) responses with status codes in the  "informational" 
([1xx](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) range, followed by exactly one  "final" response with a status code in one of the other ranges.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [15.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Overview of Status Codes](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        
   The status codes listed below are defined in this specification.   The reason 
phrases listed here are only recommendations -- they can be   replaced by local equivalents 
or left out altogether without affecting the   protocol.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Responses with status codes that are defined as heuristically cacheable   (e.g., 
200, 203, 204, 206, 300, 301, 308, 404, 405, 410, 414, and 501 in this   specification) 
can be reused by a cache with heuristic expiration unless   otherwise indicated by 
the method definition or explicit cache controls   [[CACHING](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")]; 
all other status codes are not heuristically cacheable.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Additional status codes, outside the scope of this specification, have been   
specified for use in HTTP. All such status codes ought to be registered   within 
the "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Status Code Registry",   as described in 
[Section 16.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [15.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Informational 1xx](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")         
               
   The 1xx (Informational) class of status code indicates an   interim response for 
communicating connection status or request progress   prior to completing the requested 
action and sending a final response.   Since HTTP/1.0 did not define any 1xx status 
codes, a server MUST NOT send   a 1xx response to an HTTP/1.0 client.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 1xx response is terminated by the end of the header section;   it cannot contain 
content or trailers.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A client MUST be able to parse one or more 1xx responses received   prior to a 
final response, even if the client does not expect one.   A user agent MAY ignore 
unexpected 1xx responses.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A proxy MUST forward 1xx responses unless the proxy itself   requested the generation 
of the 1xx response. For example, if a   proxy adds an "Expect: 100-continue" header 
field when it forwards a request,   then it need not forward the corresponding [100 (Continue)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   response(s).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.2.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[100 Continue](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 100 (Continue) status code indicates that the initial   part of a request 
has been received and has not yet been rejected by the   server. The server intends 
to send a final response after the request has   been fully received and acted upon.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   When the request contains an [Expect](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field that   includes a [100-continue](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") expectation, the 100 response   indicates that the server wishes to receive the request content,   as described in [Section 10.1.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").  The client   ought to continue sending the request and discard the 100 response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   If the request did not contain an [Expect](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field   containing the [100-continue](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") expectation,   the client can simply discard this interim response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.2.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[101 Switching Protocols](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 101 (Switching Protocols) status code indicates that the   server understands 
and is willing to comply with the client's request,   via the [Upgrade](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field ([Section 7.8](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")), for   a change in the application protocol being used on this connection.   The server
MUST generate an Upgrade header field in the response that   indicates which protocol(s) 
will be in effect after this response.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   It is assumed that the server will only agree to switch protocols when   it is 
advantageous to do so. For example, switching to a newer version of   HTTP might 
be advantageous over older versions, and switching to a   real-time, synchronous 
protocol might be advantageous when delivering   resources that use such features.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [15.3. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Successful 2xx](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")         
               
   The 2xx (Successful) class of status code indicates that   the client's request 
was successfully received, understood, and accepted.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.3.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[200 OK](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 200 (OK) status code indicates that the request has   succeeded. The content 
sent in a 200 response depends on the request   method. For the methods defined by 
this specification, the intended meaning   of the content can be summarized as:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

| [Table 6](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") |
||----------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------:|
| -                                        | -              |
|                                          |
|                                          | GET            | the [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")                                                                      |  |
|                                          | HEAD           | the [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), like GET, but without          transferring the representation data |  |
|                                          | POST           | the status of, or results obtained from, the action                                                                       |  |
|                                          | PUT, DELETE    | the status of the action                                                                                                  |  |
|                                          | OPTIONS        | communication options for the target resource                                                                             |  |
|                                          | TRACE          | the request message as received by the server returning the          trace                                                |  |

   Aside from responses to CONNECT, a 200 response is expected to contain   message 
content unless the message framing explicitly indicates that the   content has zero 
length. If some aspect of the request indicates a   preference for no content upon 
success, the origin server ought to send a   204 (No Content) response instead.  
 For CONNECT, there is no content because the successful result is a   tunnel, which 
begins immediately after the 200 response header section.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 200 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   In 200 responses to GET or HEAD, an origin server SHOULD send any   available 
validator fields ([Section 8.8](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) for the   [selected representation](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), with both a strong entity tag and   a [Last-Modified](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") date being preferred.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   In 200 responses to state-changing methods, any validator fields   ([Section 8.8](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) sent in the response convey the   current validators for the new representation formed as a result of   successfully applying the request semantics. Note that the PUT method   ([Section 9.3.4](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")) has additional requirements that might preclude   sending such validators.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.3.2. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[201 Created](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 201 (Created) status code indicates that the request has   been fulfilled 
and has resulted in one or more new resources being created.   The primary resource 
created by the request is identified by either a   [Location](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") header field in the response or, if no   [Location](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") header field is received, by the target URI.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   The 201 response content typically describes and links to the resource(s)   created. 
Any validator fields ([Section 8.8](rfc-local.css "section-abstract"))   sent in the response convey the current validators for a new   representation created by the request. Note that the PUT method   ([Section 9.3.4](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")) has additional requirements that might preclude   sending such validators.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.3.3. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[202 Accepted](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 202 (Accepted) status code indicates that the request   has been accepted 
for processing, but the processing has not been   completed. The request might or 
might not eventually be acted upon, as it   might be disallowed when processing actually 
takes place. There is no   facility in HTTP for re-sending a status code from an 
asynchronous   operation.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   The 202 response is intentionally noncommittal. Its purpose is to   allow a server 
to accept a request for some other process (perhaps a   batch-oriented process that 
is only run once per day) without   requiring that the user agent's connection to 
the server persist   until the process is completed. The representation sent with 
this   response ought to describe the request's current status and point to   (or 
embed) a status monitor that can provide the user with an estimate of   when the 
request will be fulfilled.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.3.4. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[203 Non-Authoritative Information](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 203 (Non-Authoritative Information) status code   indicates that the request 
was successful but the enclosed content has been   modified from that of the origin 
server's [200 (OK)](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") response   by a transforming proxy ([Section 7.7](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")). This status code allows the   proxy to notify recipients when a transformation has been applied, since   that knowledge might impact later decisions regarding the content. For   example, future cache validation requests for the content might only be   applicable along the same request path (through the same proxies).[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   A 203 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"section-abstract")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.3.5. ](https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/draft-ietf-httpbis-semantics-19 "section-abstract")[204 No Content](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")           
         
   The 204 (No Content) status code indicates that the server   has successfully 
fulfilled the request and that there is no additional   content to send in the response 
content. Metadata in the response   header fields refer to the [target resource](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract") and its   [selected representation](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract") after the requested action was applied.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

   For example, if a 204 status code is received in response to a PUT   request and
