the response contains an [ETag](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2818 "section-abstract") field, then   the PUT was successful and the ETag field value contains the entity tag for   the new representation of that target resource.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7230 "section-abstract")

   The 204 response allows a server to indicate that the action has been   successfully 
applied to the target resource, while implying that the   user agent does not need 
to traverse away from its current "document view"   (if any).  The server assumes 
that the user agent will provide some   indication of the success to its user, in 
accord with its own interface,   and apply any new or updated metadata in the response 
to its active   representation.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3864 "section-abstract")

   For example, a 204 status code is commonly used with document editing   interfaces 
corresponding to a "save" action, such that the document   being saved remains available 
to the user for editing. It is also   frequently used with interfaces that expect 
automated data transfers   to be prevalent, such as within distributed version control 
systems.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

   A 204 response is terminated by the end of the header section;   it cannot contain 
content or trailers.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

   A 204 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](#abstract "section-abstract") of [[CACHING](#abstract
"section-abstract")]).[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.3.6. ](#section-abstract-1 "text-left")[205 Reset Content](#section-abstract-1 "text-left")           
         
   The 205 (Reset Content) status code indicates that the   server has fulfilled 
the request and desires that the user agent reset the   "document view", which caused 
the request to be sent, to its original state   as received from the origin server.[¶](#section-abstract-2 "text-left")

   This response is intended to support a common data entry use case where   the 
user receives content that supports data entry (a form, notepad,   canvas, etc.), 
enters or manipulates data in that space, causes the entered   data to be submitted 
in a request, and then the data entry mechanism is   reset for the next entry so 
that the user can easily initiate another   input action.[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-1 "text-left")

   Since the 205 status code implies that no additional content will be   provided, 
a server MUST NOT generate content in a 205 response.[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")

          
#### [15.3.7. ](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")[206 Partial Content](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")           
         
   The 206 (Partial Content) status code indicates that the   server is successfully 
fulfilling a range request for the target resource   by transferring one or more 
parts of the   [selected representation](#section-boilerplate.1-3 "text-left").[¶](#name-copyright-notice "text-left")

   A server that supports range requests ([Section 14](#name-copyright-notice "text-left")) will   usually attempt to satisfy all of the requested ranges, since sending   less data will likely result in another client request for the remainder.   However, a server might want to send only a subset of the data requested   for reasons of its own, such as temporary unavailability, cache efficiency,   load balancing, etc. Since a 206 response is self-descriptive, the client   can still understand a response that only partially satisfies its range   request.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")

   A client MUST inspect a 206 response's [Content-Type](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left") and   [Content-Range](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left") field(s) to determine what parts are enclosed   and whether additional requests are needed.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")

   A server that generates a 206 response MUST generate the following   header fields, 
in addition to those required in the subsections below,   if the field would   have 
been sent in a [200 (OK)](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left") response to the same request:   [Date](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left"), Cache-Control, [ETag](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left"),   Expires, [Content-Location](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left"), and   [Vary](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left").[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")

   A [Content-Length](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left") header field present in a 206 response   indicates the number of octets in the content of this message, which is   usually not the complete length of the selected representation.   Each [Content-Range](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left") header field includes information about the   selected representation's complete length.[¶](# "text-left")

   A sender that generates a 206 response to a request with an [If-Range](#name-table-of-contents "text-left")   header field
SHOULD NOT generate other representation header   fields beyond those required because 
the client   already has a prior response containing those header fields.   Otherwise, 
a sender MUST generate all of the representation header   fields that would have 
been sent in a [200 (OK)](#section-1.1 "text-left") response   to the same request.[¶](#section-1.1 "text-left")

   A 206 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](#section-1.2 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-1.2
"text-left")]).[¶](#section-1.2 "text-left")

            
##### [15.3.7.1. ](#section-1.3 "text-left")[Single Part](#section-1.3 "text-left")            
   If a single part is being transferred, the server generating the 206   response 
MUST generate a [Content-Range](#section-1.4 "text-left") header field,   describing what range of the selected representation is enclosed, and a   content consisting of the range. For example:[¶](#section-2 "text-left")

```HTTP/1.1 206 Partial ContentDate: Wed, 15 Nov 1995 06:25:24 GMTLast-Modified:
Wed, 15 Nov 1995 04:58:08 GMTContent-Range: bytes 21010-47021/47022Content-Length:
26012Content-Type: image/gif... 26012 bytes of partial image data ...```
[¶](#section-2.1 "text-left")


##### [15.3.7.2. ](#section-2.2 "text-left")[Multiple Parts](#section-2.2 "text-left")            
If multiple parts are being transferred, the server generating the 206   response
MUST generate "multipart/byteranges" content, as defined   in [Section 14.6](#section-2.3 "text-left"), and a   [Content-Type](#section-2.3 "text-left") header field containing the   "multipart/byteranges" media type and its required boundary parameter.   To avoid confusion with single-part responses, a server
MUST NOT generate   a [Content-Range](#section-2.4 "text-left") header field in the HTTP header section of a   multiple part response (this field will be sent in each part instead).[¶](#section-2.5 "text-left")

Within the header area of each body part in the multipart content, the   server
MUST generate a [Content-Range](#section-3 "text-left") header field   corresponding to the range being enclosed in that body part.   If the selected representation would have had a [Content-Type](#name-terminology-and-core-concep "text-left")   header field in a [200 (OK)](#section-3.1 "text-left") response, the server
SHOULD   generate that same [Content-Type](#section-3.1 "text-left") header field in the header area of   each body part. For example:[¶](#section-3.2 "text-left")

```HTTP/1.1 206 Partial ContentDate: Wed, 15 Nov 1995 06:25:24 GMTLast-Modified: 
Wed, 15 Nov 1995 04:58:08 GMTContent-Length: 1741Content-Type: multipart/byteranges; 
boundary=THIS_STRING_SEPARATES--THIS_STRING_SEPARATESContent-Type: application/pdfContent-Range: 
bytes 500-999/8000...the first range...--THIS_STRING_SEPARATESContent-Type:
application/pdfContent-Range: bytes 7000-7999/8000...the second range--THIS_STRING_SEPARATES--```
[¶](#section-3.4 "text-left")
   When multiple ranges are requested, a server MAY coalesce any of the   ranges 
that overlap, or that are separated by a gap that is smaller than the   overhead 
of sending multiple parts, regardless of the order in which the   corresponding range-spec 
appeared in the received [Range](#section-3.5 "text-left")   header field. Since the typical overhead between each part of a   "multipart/byteranges" is around 80 bytes, depending on the selected   representation's media type and the chosen boundary parameter length, it   can be less efficient to transfer many small disjoint parts than it is to   transfer the entire selected representation.[¶](#section-3.7 "text-left")

   A server MUST NOT generate a multipart response to a request for a single   range, 
since a client that does not request multiple parts might not   support multipart 
responses. However, a server MAY generate a   "multipart/byteranges" response with 
only a single body part if multiple   ranges were requested and only one range was 
found to be satisfiable or   only one range remained after coalescing.   A client 
that cannot process a "multipart/byteranges" response MUST NOT   generate a request 
that asks for multiple ranges.[¶](#section-4 "text-left")

   A server that generates a multipart response SHOULD send   the parts in the same 
order that the corresponding range-spec appeared   in the received [Range](#name-identifiers-in-http "text-left") header field, excluding those ranges   that were deemed unsatisfiable or that were coalesced into other ranges.   A client that receives a multipart response
MUST inspect the   [Content-Range](#name-uri-references "text-left") header field present in each body part in   order to determine which range is contained in that body part; a client   cannot rely on receiving the same ranges that it requested, nor the same   order that it requested.[¶](#name-http-related-uri-schemes "text-left")

            
##### [15.3.7.3. ](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")[Combining Parts](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")            
   A response might transfer only a subrange of a representation if the   connection 
closed prematurely or if the request used one or more Range   specifications.  After 
several such transfers, a client might have   received several ranges of the same 
representation.  These ranges can only   be safely combined if they all have in common 
the same strong validator   ([Section 8.8.1](#section-4.2.3 "text-left")).[¶](#section-4.2.3 "text-left")

   A client that has received multiple partial responses to GET requests on a   target 
resource MAY combine those responses into a larger continuous   range if they share 
the same strong validator.[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")

   If the most recent response is an incomplete [200 (OK)](#section-4.2.5 "text-left")   response, then the header fields of that response are used for any   combined response and replace those of the matching stored responses.[¶](#name-https-references-with-fragm "text-left")

   If the most recent response is a [206 (Partial Content)](#section-4.3 "text-left")   response and at least one of the matching stored responses is a   [200 (OK)](#section-4.3 "text-left"), then the combined response header fields consist   of the most recent 200 response's header fields. If all of the matching   stored responses are 206 responses, then the stored response with the most   recent header fields is used as the source of header fields for the   combined response, except that the client
MUST use other header fields   provided in the new response, aside from [Content-Range](#section-4.3.2 "text-left"), to   replace all instances of the corresponding header fields in the stored   response.[¶](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")

   The combined response content consists of the union of partial content   ranges 
within the new response and all of the matching stored responses.   If the union 
consists of the entire range of the representation, then the   client MUST process 
the combined response as if it were a complete   [200 (OK)](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") response, including a [Content-Length](#section-4.3.4 "text-left")   header field that reflects the complete length.   Otherwise, the client
MUST process the set of continuous ranges as one of   the following:   an incomplete 
[200 (OK)](#section-4.3.5 "text-left") response if the combined response is   a prefix of the representation,   a single [206 (Partial Content)](#section-4.3.5 "text-left") response containing   "multipart/byteranges" content, or   multiple [206 (Partial Content)](#section-5 "text-left") responses, each with one   continuous range that is indicated by a [Content-Range](#section-5 "text-left") header   field.[¶](#name-fields "text-left")

        
### [15.4. ](#section-5.1 "text-left")[Redirection 3xx](#name-field-names "text-left")         
               
   The 3xx (Redirection) class of status code indicates that   further action needs 
to be taken by the user agent in order to fulfill the   request. There are several 
types of redirects:[¶](#section-5.3 "text-left")

               1.     Redirects that indicate this resource might be available at a    different URI, as provided by the [Location](#section-5.4 "text-left") header field,    as in the status codes [301 (Moved Permanently)](#name-field-limits "text-left"),    [302 (Found)](#section-5.5 "text-left"), [307 (Temporary Redirect)](#section-5.5 "text-left"), and    [308 (Permanent Redirect)](#section-5.5 "text-left").[¶](#name-field-values "text-left")
          2.     Redirection that offers a choice among matching resources capable    of representing this resource, as in the    [300 (Multiple Choices)](#section-5.6 "text-left") status code.[¶](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")
          3.     Redirection to a different resource, identified by the    [Location](#section-5.6.1 "text-left") header field, that can represent an indirect    response to the request, as in the [303 (See Other)](#name-lists-rule-abnf-extension "text-left")    status code.[¶](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")
          4.     Redirection to a previously stored result, as in the    [304 (Not Modified)](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left") status code.[¶](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")
        
          
                  **Note:** In HTTP/1.0, the status codes [301 (Moved Permanently)](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")    and [302 (Found)](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left") were originally defined as method-preserving   
([[HTTP/1.0](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")], [Section 9.3](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")) 
to match their implementation    at CERN; [303 (See Other)](#section-5.6.2 "text-left") was defined for a redirection that    changed its method to GET. However, early user agents split on whether to    redirect POST requests as POST (according to then-current specification)    or as GET (the safer alternative when redirected to a different site).    Prevailing practice eventually converged on changing the method to GET.    [307 (Temporary Redirect)](#section-5.6.4 "text-left") and    [308 (Permanent Redirect)](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")           
[[RFC7538](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")] were    later added to unambiguously indicate 
method-preserving redirects, and status codes    [301](#section-5.6.5 "text-left") and [302](#section-5.6.5 "text-left") have been adjusted to allow a POST    request to be redirected as GET.[¶](#section-5.6.5 "text-left")

   If a [Location](#section-5.6.6 "text-left") header field   ([Section 10.2.2](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")) is provided, the user agent
MAY   automatically redirect its request to the URI referenced by the Location  
 field value, even if the specific status code is not understood.   Automatic redirection 
needs to be done with care for methods not known to be   [safe](#section-5.6.7 "text-left"), as defined in [Section 9.2.1](#name-date-time-formats "text-left"), since   the user might not wish to redirect an unsafe request.[¶](#section-6 "text-left")

   When automatically following a redirected request, the user agent SHOULD   resend 
the original request message with the following modifications:[¶](#section-6.1 "text-left")

               1.                   Replace the target URI with the URI referenced by the redirection response's      [Location](#section-6.2 "text-left") header field value after resolving it relative to the original      request's target URI.[¶](#section-6.2 "text-left")
          2.                   Remove header fields that were automatically generated by the implementation,      replacing them with updated values as appropriate to the new request. This      includes:[¶](#name-header-fields "text-left")

                     1. Connection-specific header fields (see [Section 7.6.1](#section-6.4 "text-left")),[¶](#name-content "text-left")
              2. Header fields specific to the client's proxy configuration,       including (but not limited to) [Proxy-Authorization](#section-6.4.1 "text-left"),[¶](#name-content-semantics "text-left")
              3. Origin-specific header fields (if any), including (but not       limited to) [Host](#section-6.4.2 "text-left"),[¶](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")
              4. Validating header fields that were added by the implementation's       cache (e.g., [If-None-Match](#section-6.5 "text-left"),       [If-Modified-Since](#section-6.5 "text-left")), and[¶](#name-trailer-fields "text-left")
              5. Resource-specific header fields, including (but not limited to)       [Referer](#section-6.5.1 "text-left"), Origin,       [Authorization](#section-6.5.1 "text-left"), and Cookie.[¶](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left")
            
          -                   Consider removing header fields that were not automatically generated by the      implementation (i.e., those present in the request because they were added      by the calling context) where there are security implications; this      includes but is not limited to [Authorization](#section-6.6 "text-left") and Cookie.[¶](#section-6.6 "text-left")
          -                   Change the request method according to the redirecting status code's      semantics, if applicable.[¶](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")
          -                   If the request method has been changed to GET or HEAD, remove      content-specific header fields, including (but not limited to)      [Content-Encoding](#section-6.6.2 "text-left"),      [Content-Language](#section-6.6.2 "text-left"), [Content-Location](#name-trailer "text-left"),      [Content-Type](#section-7 "text-left"), [Content-Length](#section-7 "text-left"),      Digest, [Last-Modified](#section-7 "text-left").[¶](#name-routing-http-messages "text-left")
        

   A client SHOULD detect and intervene in cyclical redirections (i.e.,   "infinite" 
redirection loops).[¶](#section-7.1 "text-left")
          
                  **Note:** An earlier version of this specification recommended 
a    maximum of five redirections ([[RFC2068](#section-7.2 "text-left")], [Section 10.3](#name-host-and-authority
"text-left")).    Content developers need to be aware that some clients might   
 implement such a fixed limitation.[¶](#section-7.3 "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.1. ](#section-7.3.1 "text-left")[300 Multiple Choices](#section-7.3.1 "text-left")           
         
   The 300 (Multiple Choices) status code indicates that the   [target resource](#section-7.3.2 "text-left") has more than one representation, each with   its own more specific identifier, and information about the alternatives is   being provided so that the user (or user agent) can select a preferred   representation by redirecting its request to one or more of those   identifiers. In other words, the server desires that the user agent engage   in reactive negotiation to select the most appropriate representation(s)   for its needs ([Section 12](#section-7.4 "text-left")).[¶](#section-7.4 "text-left")

   If the server has a preferred choice, the server SHOULD generate a   [Location](#name-rejecting-misdirected-reque "text-left") header field containing a preferred choice's URI   reference. The user agent
MAY use the Location field value for automatic   redirection.[¶](#section-7.5 "text-left")

   For request methods other than HEAD, the server SHOULD generate content   in the 
300 response containing a list of representation metadata and URI   reference(s) 
from which the user or user agent can choose the one most   preferred. The user agent 
MAY make a selection from that list   automatically if it understands the provided 
media type. A specific format   for automatic selection is not defined by this specification 
because HTTP   tries to remain orthogonal to the definition of its content.   In 
practice, the representation is provided in some easily parsed format   believed 
to be acceptable to the user agent, as determined by shared design   or content negotiation, 
or in some commonly accepted hypertext format.[¶](#name-max-forwards "text-left")

   A 300 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](#section-7.6.3 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-7.7
"text-left")]).[¶](#section-7.7 "text-left")
            
                     **Note:** The original proposal for the 300 status code defined 
the URI header field as   providing a list of alternative representations, such that 
it would be   usable for 200, 300, and 406 responses and be transferred in responses 
to   the HEAD method. However, lack of deployment and disagreement over syntax   
led to both URI and Alternates (a subsequent proposal) being dropped from   this 
specification. It is possible to communicate the list as a   Link header field value 
[[RFC8288](#section-8 "text-left")] whose members have a relationship of   "alternate", 
though deployment is a chicken-and-egg problem.[¶](#section-8.1 "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.2. ](#name-representation-data "text-left")[301 Moved Permanently](#section-8.2 "text-left")           
         
   The 301 (Moved Permanently) status code indicates that the   [target resource](#section-8.3 "text-left") has been assigned a new permanent URI and   any future references to this resource ought to use one of the enclosed   URIs. The server is suggesting that a user agent with link-editing capability   can permanently replace references to the target URI with one of the   new references sent by the server. However, this suggestion is usually   ignored unless the user agent is actively editing references   (e.g., engaged in authoring content), the connection is secured, and   the origin server is a trusted authority for the content being edited.[¶](#section-8.3.2 "text-left")

   The server SHOULD generate a [Location](#name-charset "text-left") header field in the   response containing a preferred URI reference for the new permanent URI.   The user agent
MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection.   The server's response 
content usually contains a short hypertext note with   a hyperlink to the new URI(s).[¶](#section-8.4 "text-left")
            
                     **Note:** For historical reasons, a user agent MAY change the 
   request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. If this    behavior 
is undesired, the [308 (Permanent Redirect)](#section-8.4.1 "text-left")    status code can be used instead.[¶](#section-8.4.1 "text-left")

   A 301 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-8.4.1.1
"text-left")]).[¶](#name-compress-coding "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.3. ](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")[302 Found](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")           
         
   The 302 (Found) status code indicates that the target   resource resides temporarily 
under a different URI. Since the redirection   might be altered on occasion, the 
client ought to continue to use the   target URI for future requests.[¶](#section-8.5 "text-left")

   The server SHOULD generate a [Location](#section-8.5 "text-left") header field in the   response containing a URI reference for the different URI.   The user agent
MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection.   The server's response 
content usually contains a short hypertext note with   a hyperlink to the different 
URI(s).[¶](#section-8.5.1 "text-left")
            
                     **Note:** For historical reasons, a user agent MAY change the 
   request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. If this    behavior 
is undesired, the [307 (Temporary Redirect)](#name-content-length "text-left")    status code can be used instead.[¶](#section-8.7 "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.4. ](#section-8.8 "text-left")[303 See Other](#section-8.8 "text-left")           
         
   The 303 (See Other) status code indicates that the server is   redirecting the 
user agent to a different resource, as indicated by a URI   in the [Location](#section-8.8.1 "text-left") header field, which is intended to provide   an indirect response to the original request. A user agent can perform a   retrieval request targeting that URI (a GET or HEAD request if using HTTP),   which might also be redirected, and present the eventual result as an   answer to the original request. Note that the new URI in the Location   header field is not considered equivalent to the target URI.[¶](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")

   This status code is applicable to any HTTP method.  It is   primarily used to 
allow the output of a POST action to redirect   the user agent to a different resource, 
since doing so provides the   information corresponding to the POST response as a 
resource that   can be separately identified, bookmarked, and cached.[¶](#section-8.8.2.2 "text-left")

   A 303 response to a GET request indicates that the origin server does not   have 
a representation of the [target resource](#section-8.8.3 "text-left") that can be   transferred by the server over HTTP. However, the   [Location](#section-8.8.3 "text-left") field value refers to a resource that is   descriptive of the target resource, such that making a retrieval request   on that other resource might result in a representation that is useful to   recipients without implying that it represents the original target resource.   Note that answers to the questions of what can be represented, what   representations are adequate, and what might be a useful description are   outside the scope of HTTP.[¶](#section-8.8.3.2 "text-left")

   Except for responses to a HEAD request, the representation of a 303   response 
ought to contain a short hypertext note with a hyperlink to the   same URI reference 
provided in the [Location](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left") header field.[¶](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.5. ](#name-example-entity-tags-varying "text-left")[304 Not Modified](#section-9 "text-left")           
         
   The 304 (Not Modified) status code indicates that a   conditional GET or HEAD 
request has been   received and would have resulted in a [200 (OK)](#section-9.1 "text-left") response   if it were not for the fact that the condition evaluated to false.   In other words, there is no need for the server to transfer a   representation of the target resource because the request indicates that   the client, which made the request conditional, already has a valid   representation; the server is therefore redirecting the client to make   use of that stored representation as if it were the content of a   [200 (OK)](#name-common-method-properties "text-left") response.[¶](#section-9.2.1 "text-left")

   The server generating a 304 response MUST generate any of the following   header 
fields that would have been sent in a [200 (OK)](#name-safe-methods "text-left")   response to the same request:[¶](#section-9.2.2 "text-left")

-               [Content-Location](#section-9.2.2 "text-left"), [Date](#name-idempotent-methods "text-left"), [ETag](#section-9.2.3 "text-left"),    and [Vary](#section-9.2.3 "text-left")[¶](#section-9.2.3 "text-left")
            -     Cache-Control and Expires (see    [[CACHING](#name-methods-and-caching "text-left")])[¶](#section-9.3 "text-left")
          

   Since the goal of a 304 response is to minimize information transfer   when the 
recipient already has one or more cached representations,   a sender SHOULD NOT generate 
representation metadata other   than the above listed fields unless said metadata 
exists for the   purpose of guiding cache updates (e.g., [Last-Modified](#section-9.3.1 "text-left") might   be useful if the response does not have an [ETag](#name-get "text-left") field).[¶](#section-9.3.2 "text-left")

   Requirements on a cache that receives a 304 response are defined in   [Section 4.3.4](#name-head "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-9.3.3
"text-left")]. If the conditional request originated with an   outbound client, 
such as a user agent with its own cache sending a   conditional GET to a shared proxy, 
then the proxy SHOULD forward the   304 response to that client.[¶](#section-9.3.4 "text-left")

   A 304 response is terminated by the end of the header section;   it cannot contain 
content or trailers.[¶](#section-9.3.5 "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.6. ](#section-9.3.5 "text-left")[305 Use Proxy](#section-9.3.6 "text-left")           
         
   The 305 (Use Proxy) status code was defined in a previous   version of this specification 
and is now deprecated ([Appendix B](#section-9.3.7 "text-left") of [[RFC7231](#section-9.3.7
"text-left")]).[¶](#name-options "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.7. ](#section-9.3.8 "text-left")[306 (Unused)](#section-9.3.8 "text-left")           
         
   The 306 status code was defined in a previous version of this   specification, 
is no longer used, and the code is reserved.[¶](#name-message-context "text-left")

          
#### [15.4.8. ](#section-10.1 "text-left")[307 Temporary Redirect](#section-10.1 "text-left")           
         
   The 307 (Temporary Redirect) status code indicates that the   [target resource](rfc9110.xml "Roy T. Fielding") resides temporarily under a different URI   and the user agent
MUST NOT change the request method if it performs an   automatic redirection to 
that URI.   Since the redirection can change over time, the client ought to continue 
  using the original target URI for future requests.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

   The server SHOULD generate a [Location](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") header field in the   response containing a URI reference for the different URI.   The user agent
MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection.   The server's response 
content usually contains a short hypertext note with   a hyperlink to the different 
URI(s).[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.4.9. ](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")[308 Permanent Redirect](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 308 (Permanent Redirect) status code indicates that the   [target resource](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css") has been assigned a new permanent URI and   any future references to this resource ought to use one of the enclosed   URIs. The server is suggesting that a user agent with link-editing capability   can permanently replace references to the target URI with one of the   new references sent by the server. However, this suggestion is usually   ignored unless the user agent is actively editing references   (e.g., engaged in authoring content), the connection is secured, and   the origin server is a trusted authority for the content being edited.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   The server SHOULD generate a [Location](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field in the   response containing a preferred URI reference for the new permanent URI.   The user agent
MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection.   The server's response 
content usually contains a short hypertext note with   a hyperlink to the new URI(s).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 308 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            
                     **Note:** This status code is much younger (June 2014) than 
its sibling codes and thus    might not be recognized everywhere. See [Section 4](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[RFC7538](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")] 
   for deployment considerations.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [15.5. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Client Error 4xx](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")         
               
   The 4xx (Client Error) class of status code indicates that   the client seems 
to have erred. Except when responding to a HEAD request,   the server SHOULD send 
a representation containing an explanation of   the error situation, and whether 
it is a temporary or permanent condition.   These status codes are applicable to 
any request method. User agents   SHOULD display any included representation to the 
user.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[400 Bad Request](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 400 (Bad Request) status code indicates that the server   cannot or will not 
process the request due to something that is perceived   to be a client error (e.g., 
malformed request syntax, invalid request   message framing, or deceptive request 
routing).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[401 Unauthorized](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 401 (Unauthorized) status code indicates that the   request has not been applied 
because it lacks valid authentication   credentials for the target resource.   The 
server generating a 401 response MUST send a   [WWW-Authenticate](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field   ([Section 11.6.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"))   containing at least one challenge applicable to the target resource.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   If the request included authentication credentials, then the 401 response   indicates 
that authorization has been refused for those credentials.   The user agent MAY repeat 
the request with a new or replaced   [Authorization](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field ([Section 11.6.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).   If the 401 response contains the same challenge as the prior response, and   the user agent has already attempted authentication at least once, then the   user agent
SHOULD present the enclosed representation to the user, since   it usually contains 
relevant diagnostic information.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.3. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[402 Payment Required](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 402 (Payment Required) status code is reserved for   future use.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.4. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[403 Forbidden](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 403 (Forbidden) status code indicates that the   server understood the request 
but refuses to fulfill it.   A server that wishes to make public why the request 
has been forbidden   can describe that reason in the response content (if any).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   If authentication credentials were provided in the request, the   server considers 
them insufficient to grant access.   The client SHOULD NOT automatically repeat the 
request with the same   credentials.   The client MAY repeat the request with new 
or different credentials.   However, a request might be forbidden for reasons unrelated 
to the   credentials.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   An origin server that wishes to "hide" the current existence of a forbidden   
[target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")            MAY instead respond with 
a status   code of [404 (Not Found)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.5. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[404 Not Found](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 404 (Not Found) status code indicates that the origin   server did not find 
a current representation for the   [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") or is not willing to disclose that one   exists. A 404 status code does not indicate whether this lack of representation   is temporary or permanent; the [410 (Gone)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") status code is   preferred over 404 if the origin server knows, presumably through some   configurable means, that the condition is likely to be permanent.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 404 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.6. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[405 Method Not Allowed](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 405 (Method Not Allowed) status code indicates that the   method received 
in the request-line is known by the origin server but   not supported by the [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").   The origin server
MUST generate an [Allow](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field in   a 405 response containing a list of the target resource's currently   supported methods.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 405 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.7. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[406 Not Acceptable](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 406 (Not Acceptable) status code indicates that the   [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") does not have a current representation that   would be acceptable to the user agent, according to the   [proactive negotiation](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header fields received in the request   ([Section 12.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")), and the server is unwilling to supply a   default representation.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   The server SHOULD generate content containing a list of available   representation 
characteristics and corresponding resource identifiers from   which the user or user 
agent can choose the one most appropriate.   A user agent MAY automatically select 
the most appropriate choice from   that list. However, this specification does not 
define any standard for   such automatic selection, as described in [Section 15.4.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.8. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[407 Proxy Authentication Required](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 407 (Proxy Authentication Required) status code is   similar to [401 (Unauthorized)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), but it indicates that the client   needs to authenticate itself in order to use a proxy for this request.   The proxy
MUST send a [Proxy-Authenticate](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field   ([Section 11.7.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) containing a challenge   applicable to that proxy for the request. The client
MAY repeat   the request with a new or replaced [Proxy-Authorization](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   header field ([Section 11.7.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.9. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[408 Request Timeout](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 408 (Request Timeout) status code indicates   that the server did not receive 
a complete request message within the time   that it was prepared to wait.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   If the client has an outstanding request in transit, it MAY repeat that   request. 
If the current connection is not usable (e.g., as it would be in   HTTP/1.1 because 
request delimitation is lost), a new connection will be   used.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.10. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[409 Conflict](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 409 (Conflict) status code indicates that the request   could not be completed 
due to a conflict with the current state of the target   resource. This code is used 
in situations where the user might be able to   resolve the conflict and resubmit 
the request. The server SHOULD generate   content that includes enough information 
for a user to recognize the   source of the conflict.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Conflicts are most likely to occur in response to a PUT request. For   example, 
if versioning were being used and the representation being PUT   included changes 
to a resource that conflict with those made by an   earlier (third-party) request, 
the origin server might use a 409 response   to indicate that it can't complete the 
request. In this case, the response   representation would likely contain information 
useful for merging the   differences based on the revision history.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.11. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[410 Gone](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 410 (Gone) status code indicates that access to the   [target resource](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") is no longer available at the origin   server and that this condition is likely to be permanent. If the origin   server does not know, or has no facility to determine, whether or not the   condition is permanent, the status code [404 (Not Found)](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")   ought to be used instead.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   The 410 response is primarily intended to assist the task of web   maintenance 
by notifying the recipient that the resource is   intentionally unavailable and that 
the server owners desire that   remote links to that resource be removed. Such an 
event is common for   limited-time, promotional services and for resources belonging 
to   individuals no longer associated with the origin server's site. It is not   
necessary to mark all permanently unavailable resources as "gone" or   to keep the 
mark for any length of time -- that is left to the   discretion of the server owner.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   A 410 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.12. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[411 Length Required](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 411 (Length Required) status code indicates that the   server refuses to accept 
the request without a defined   [Content-Length](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") ([Section 8.6](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).   The client
MAY repeat the request if it adds a valid Content-Length   header field containing 
the length of the request content.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [15.5.13. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[412 Precondition Failed](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")           
         
   The 412 (Precondition Failed) status code indicates that one   or more conditions 
given in the request header fields evaluated to false   when tested on the server 
([Section 13](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")). This   response status code allows the client to place preconditions on the   current resource state (its current representations and metadata) and,   thus, prevent the request method from being applied if the target resource   is in an unexpected state.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.5.14. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[413 Content Too Large](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 413 (Content Too Large) status code indicates   that the server is refusing 
to process a request because the request   content is larger than the server is willing 
or able to process.   The server MAY terminate the request, if the protocol version 
in use   allows it; otherwise, the server MAY close the connection.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   If the condition is temporary, the server SHOULD generate a   [Retry-After](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") header field to indicate that it is temporary   and after what time the client
MAY try again.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.5.15. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[414 URI Too Long](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 414 (URI Too Long) status code indicates that the server   is refusing to 
service the request because the   target URI is longer than the server is willing 
to   interpret. This rare condition is only likely to occur when a client has   improperly 
converted a POST request to a GET request with long query   information, when the 
client has descended into an infinite loop of   redirection (e.g., a redirected URI 
prefix that points to a suffix of   itself) or when the server is under attack by 
a client attempting to   exploit potential security holes.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   A 414 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css
"section-abstract")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.5.16. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[415 Unsupported Media Type](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")           
         
   The 415 (Unsupported Media Type) status code indicates that   the origin server 
is refusing to service the request because the content is   in a format not supported 
by this method on the [target resource](rfc-local.css "section-abstract").[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   The format problem might be due to the request's indicated   [Content-Type](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") or [Content-Encoding](rfc-local.css "section-abstract"), or as a   result of inspecting the data directly.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   If the problem was caused by an unsupported content coding, the   [Accept-Encoding](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") response header field   ([Section 12.5.3](urn:issn:2070-1721 "section-abstract")) ought to be   used to indicate which (if any) content codings would have been accepted   in the request.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

   On the other hand, if the cause was an unsupported media type, the   [Accept](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract") response header field ([Section 12.5.1](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract"))   can be used to indicate which media types would have been accepted   in the request.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

          
#### [15.5.17. ](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")[416 Range Not Satisfiable](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")           
         
   The 416 (Range Not Satisfiable) status code indicates that   the set of ranges 
in the request's [Range](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7230 "section-abstract") header field   ([Section 14.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7231 "section-abstract")) has been rejected either because none of   the requested ranges are satisfiable or because the client has requested   an excessive number of small or overlapping ranges (a potential denial of   service attack).[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7538 "section-abstract")

   Each range unit defines what is required for its own range sets to be   satisfiable. 
For example, [Section 14.1.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7694 "section-abstract") defines what makes   a bytes range set satisfiable.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3864 "section-abstract")

   A server that generates a 416 response to a byte-range request SHOULD   generate 
a [Content-Range](#abstract "section-abstract") header field   specifying the current length of the selected representation   ([Section 14.4](#abstract "section-abstract")).[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

   For example:[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

```HTTP/1.1 416 Range Not SatisfiableDate: Fri, 20 Jan 2012 15:41:54 GMTContent-Range:
bytes \*/47022```
[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")            
**Note:** Because servers are free to ignore [Range](#abstract "text-left"), many    implementations will respond with the entire selected representation    in a [200 (OK)](#section-abstract-1 "text-left") response. That is partly because    most clients are prepared to receive a [200 (OK)](#section-abstract-1 "text-left") to    complete the task (albeit less efficiently) and partly because clients    might not stop making an invalid range request until they have received    a complete representation. Thus, clients cannot depend on receiving a    [416 (Range Not Satisfiable)](#section-abstract-1 "text-left") response even when it is most    appropriate.[¶](#section-abstract-2 "text-left")


#### [15.5.18. ](#section-abstract-2 "text-left")[417 Expectation Failed](#name-status-of-this-memo "text-left")           

The 417 (Expectation Failed) status code indicates that the   expectation given
in the request's [Expect](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left") header field   ([Section 10.1.1](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")) could not be met by at least one of the   inbound servers.[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")


#### [15.5.19. ](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")[418 (Unused)](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")           

[[RFC2324](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110 "text-left")]
was an April 1 RFC that lampooned the various ways   HTTP was abused; one such abuse
was the definition of an   application-specific 418 status code, which has been deployed
as a joke   often enough for the code to be unusable for any future use.[¶](#name-copyright-notice "text-left")

Therefore, the 418 status code is reserved in the IANA HTTP Status Code   Registry.
This indicates that the status code cannot be assigned to other   applications currently.
If future circumstances require its use (e.g.,   exhaustion of 4NN status codes),
it can be re-assigned to another use.[¶](https://trustee.ietf.org/license-info "text-left")


#### [15.5.20. ](https://trustee.ietf.org/license-info "text-left")[421 Misdirected Request](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")           

The 421 (Misdirected Request) status code indicates that the request was   directed
at a server that is unable or unwilling to produce an   authoritative response for
the target URI. An origin server (or gateway   acting on behalf of the origin server)
sends 421 to reject a target URI   that does not match an [origin](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left") for which the server has been   configured ([Section 4.3.1](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")) or does not match the connection   context over which the request was received   ([Section 7.4](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")).[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")

A client that receives a 421 (Misdirected Request) response MAY retry the   request,
whether or not the request method is idempotent, over a different   connection, such
as a fresh connection specific to the target resource's   origin, or via an alternative
service [[ALTSVC](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")].[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")

A proxy MUST NOT generate a 421 response.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")


#### [15.5.21. ](#name-table-of-contents "text-left")[422 Unprocessable Content](#section-1 "text-left")           

The 422 (Unprocessable Content) status code indicates that the server   understands
the content type of the request content (hence a   [415 (Unsupported Media Type)](#section-1.1 "text-left") status code is inappropriate),   and the syntax of the request content is correct, but it was unable to process   the contained instructions. For example, this status code can be sent if   an XML request content contains well-formed (i.e., syntactically correct), but   semantically erroneous XML instructions.[¶](#section-1.2 "text-left")


#### [15.5.22. ](#section-1.3 "text-left")[426 Upgrade Required](#section-1.3 "text-left")           

The 426 (Upgrade Required) status code indicates that the   server refuses to
perform the request using the current protocol but might   be willing to do so after
the client upgrades to a different protocol.   The server MUST send an [Upgrade](#section-2 "text-left") header field in a 426   response to indicate the required protocol(s) ([Section 7.8](#section-2 "text-left")).[¶](#section-2 "text-left")

Example:[¶](#section-2.1 "text-left")

```HTTP/1.1 426 Upgrade RequiredUpgrade: HTTP/3.0Connection: UpgradeContent-Length: 
53Content-Type: text/plainThis service requires use of the HTTP/3.0 protocol.```
[¶](#section-2.2 "text-left")

        
### [15.6. ](#name-requirements-notation "text-left")[Server Error 5xx](#section-2.3 "text-left")         
               
   The 5xx (Server Error) class of status code indicates that   the server is aware 
that it has erred or is incapable of performing the   requested method.   Except 
when responding to a HEAD request, the server SHOULD send a   representation containing 
an explanation of the error situation, and   whether it is a temporary or permanent 
condition.   A user agent SHOULD display any included representation to the user. 
  These status codes are applicable to any request method.[¶](#section-3 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.1. ](#section-3 "text-left")[500 Internal Server Error](#name-terminology-and-core-concep "text-left")           
         
   The 500 (Internal Server Error) status code indicates that   the server encountered 
an unexpected condition that prevented it from   fulfilling the request.[¶](#section-3.2 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.2. ](#name-representations "text-left")[501 Not Implemented](#section-3.3 "text-left")           
         
   The 501 (Not Implemented) status code indicates that the   server does not support 
the functionality required to fulfill the request.   This is the appropriate response 
when the server does not recognize the   request method and is not capable of supporting 
it for any resource.[¶](#section-3.5 "text-left")

   A 501 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by 
  the method definition or explicit cache controls (see [Section 4.2.2](#name-user-agents "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-3.6
"text-left")]).[¶](#section-3.6 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.3. ](#section-3.7 "text-left")[502 Bad Gateway](#section-3.7 "text-left")           
         
   The 502 (Bad Gateway) status code indicates that the server,   while acting as 
a gateway or proxy, received an invalid response from an   inbound server it accessed 
while attempting to fulfill the request.[¶](#section-3.9 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.4. ](#name-example-message-exchange "text-left")[503 Service Unavailable](#section-4 "text-left")           
         
   The 503 (Service Unavailable) status code indicates that the   server is currently 
unable to handle the request due to a temporary overload   or scheduled maintenance, 
which will likely be alleviated after some delay.   The server MAY send a [Retry-After](#section-4.1 "text-left") header field   ([Section 10.2.3](#section-4.2 "text-left")) to suggest an appropriate   amount of time for the client to wait before retrying the request.[¶](#section-4.2 "text-left")
            
                     **Note:** The existence of the 503 status code does not imply 
that a    server has to use it when becoming overloaded. Some servers might    simply 
refuse the connection.[¶](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.5. ](#section-4.2.2 "text-left")[504 Gateway Timeout](#section-4.2.2 "text-left")           
         
   The 504 (Gateway Timeout) status code indicates that the   server, while acting 
as a gateway or proxy, did not receive a timely   response from an upstream server 
it needed to access in order to   complete the request.[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")

          
#### [15.6.6. ](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")[505 HTTP Version Not Supported](#name-deprecation-of-userinfo-in- "text-left")           
         
   The 505 (HTTP Version Not Supported) status code indicates   that the server does 
not support, or refuses to support, the major version   of HTTP that was used in 
the request message. The server is indicating that   it is unable or unwilling to 
complete the request using the same major   version as the client, as described in 
[Section 2.5](#section-4.3 "text-left"), other than with this   error message. The server SHOULD 
generate a representation for the 505   response that describes why that version 
is not supported and what other   protocols are supported by that server.[¶](#section-4.3.1 "text-left")

      
## [16. ](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")[Extending HTTP](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")      
   HTTP defines a number of generic extension points that can be used to   introduce 
capabilities to the protocol without introducing a new version,   including methods, 
status codes, field names, and further extensibility   points within defined fields, 
such as authentication schemes and   cache directives (see Cache-Control extensions 
in [Section 5.2.3](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-4.3.4 "text-left")]). 
Because the semantics of HTTP are   not versioned, these extension points are persistent; 
the version of the   protocol in use does not affect their semantics.[¶](#section-4.3.5 "text-left")

   Version-independent extensions are discouraged from depending on or   interacting 
with the specific version of the protocol in use. When this is   unavoidable, careful 
consideration needs to be given to how the extension   can interoperate across versions.[¶](#section-5 "text-left")

   Additionally, specific versions of HTTP might have their own extensibility   points, 
such as transfer codings in HTTP/1.1 ([Section 6.1](#section-5.1 "text-left") of [[HTTP/1.1](#section-5.1
"text-left")]) and HTTP/2 SETTINGS or frame types   ([[HTTP/2](#name-field-names "text-left")]). 
These extension points are specific to the   version of the protocol they occur within.[¶](#section-5.2 "text-left")

   Version-specific extensions cannot override or modify the semantics of   a version-independent 
mechanism or extension point (like a method or   header field) without explicitly 
being allowed by that protocol element. For   example, the CONNECT method ([Section 9.3.6](#section-5.3 "text-left")) allows this.[¶](#section-5.3 "text-left")

   These guidelines assure that the protocol operates correctly and   predictably, 
even when parts of the path implement different versions of   HTTP.[¶](#section-5.4 "text-left")

        
### [16.1. ](#section-5.5 "text-left")[Method Extensibility](#section-5.5 "text-left")        

          
#### [16.1.1. ](#section-5.6 "text-left")[Method Registry](#section-5.6 "text-left")          
  The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Method Registry", maintained by  IANA at 
<[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-methods](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")>, 
 registers [method](#section-5.6.1 "text-left") names.[¶](#name-lists-rule-abnf-extension "text-left")

  HTTP method registrations MUST include the following fields:[¶](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")

- Method Name (see [Section 9](#name-sender-requirements "text-left"))[¶](#name-sender-requirements "text-left")
            - Safe ("yes" or "no", see [Section 9.2.1](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")
            - Idempotent ("yes" or "no", see [Section 9.2.2](#section-5.6.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")
            - Pointer to specification text[¶](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")
          

  Values to be added to this namespace require IETF Review  (see [[RFC8126](#section-5.6.3 "text-left")], [Section 4.8](#section-5.6.3
"text-left")).[¶](#name-whitespace "text-left")

          
#### [16.1.2. ](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")[Considerations for New Methods](#name-quoted-strings "text-left")          
   Standardized methods are generic; that is, they are potentially   applicable to 
any resource, not just one particular media type, kind of   resource, or application. 
As such, it is preferred that new methods   be registered in a document that isn't 
specific to a single application or   data format, since orthogonal technologies 
deserve orthogonal specification.[¶](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")

   Since message parsing ([Section 6](#section-5.6.7 "text-left")) needs to be   independent of method   semantics (aside from responses to HEAD), definitions of new methods   cannot change the parsing algorithm or prohibit the presence of content   on either the request or the response message.   Definitions of new methods can specify that only a zero-length content   is allowed by requiring a Content-Length header field with a value of "0".[¶](#section-6 "text-left")

   Likewise, new methods cannot use the special host:port and asterisk forms of  
 request target that are allowed for [CONNECT](#section-6.1 "text-left") and   [OPTIONS](#section-6.1 "text-left"), respectively ([Section 7.1](#section-6.1 "text-left")).   A full URI in absolute form is needed for the target URI, which means either   the request target needs to be sent in absolute form or the target URI will   be reconstructed from the request context in the same way it is for other   methods.[¶](#name-control-data "text-left")

   A new method definition needs to indicate whether it is safe ([Section 9.2.1](#section-6.3 "text-left")), idempotent ([Section 9.2.2](#section-6.3 "text-left")),   cacheable ([Section 9.2.3](#name-header-fields "text-left")), what   semantics are to be associated with the request content (if any), and what   refinements the method makes to header field or status code semantics.   If the new method is cacheable, its definition ought to describe how, and   under what conditions, a cache can store a response and use it to satisfy a   subsequent request.   The new method ought to describe whether it can be made conditional   ([Section 13.1](#section-6.4.1 "text-left")) and, if so, how a server responds   when the condition is false.   Likewise, if the new method might have some use for partial response   semantics ([Section 14.2](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")), it ought to document this, too.[¶](#name-identifying-content "text-left")
            
                     **Note:** Avoid defining a method name that starts with "M-", 
since that    prefix might be misinterpreted as having the semantics assigned to 
it    by [[RFC2774](#name-trailer-fields "text-left")].[¶](#section-6.5.1 "text-left")

        
### [16.2. ](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left")[Status Code Extensibility](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")        

          
#### [16.2.1. ](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")[Status Code Registry](#name-processing-trailer-fields "text-left")          
   The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Status Code Registry", maintained   by 
IANA at <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-status-codes](#section-6.6 "text-left")>, 
  registers status code numbers.[¶](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")

   A registration MUST include the following fields:[¶](#name-date "text-left")

- Status Code (3 digits)[¶](#section-6.6.2 "text-left")
            - Short Description[¶](#name-trailer "text-left")
            - Pointer to specification text[¶](#section-7 "text-left")
          

   Values to be added to the HTTP status code namespace require IETF Review   (see 
[[RFC8126](#section-7.1 "text-left")], [Section 4.8](#section-7.1 "text-left")).[¶](#section-7.1 "text-left")

          
#### [16.2.2. ](#section-7.2 "text-left")[Considerations for New Status Codes](#section-7.2 "text-left")          
   When it is necessary to express semantics for a response that are not   defined 
by current status codes, a new status code can be registered.   Status codes are 
generic; they are potentially applicable to any resource,   not just one particular 
media type, kind of resource, or application of   HTTP. As such, it is preferred 
that new status codes be registered in a   document that isn't specific to a single 
application.[¶](#section-7.3.1 "text-left")

   New status codes are required to fall under one of the categories   defined in 
[Section 15](#section-7.3.2 "text-left"). To allow existing parsers to   process the response message, new status codes cannot disallow content,   although they can mandate a zero-length content.[¶](#name-to-a-proxy "text-left")

   Proposals for new status codes that are not yet widely deployed ought to   avoid 
allocating a specific number for the code until there is clear   consensus that it 
will be registered; instead, early drafts can use a   notation such as "4NN", or 
"3N0" .. "3N9", to indicate the class   of the proposed status code(s) without consuming 
a number prematurely.[¶](#section-7.4 "text-left")

   The definition of a new status code ought to explain the request   conditions 
that would cause a response containing that status code (e.g.,   combinations of 
request header fields and/or method(s)) along with any   dependencies on response 
header fields (e.g., what fields are required,   what fields can modify the semantics, 
and what field semantics are   further refined when used with the new status code).[¶](#section-7.6 "text-left")

   By default, a status code applies only to the request corresponding to the   response 
it occurs within. If a status code applies to a larger scope of   applicability -- 
for example, all requests to the resource in question or   all requests to a server 
-- this must be explicitly specified. When doing   so, it should be noted that not 
all clients can be expected to   consistently apply a larger scope because they might 
not understand the   new status code.[¶](#section-7.6.2 "text-left")

   The definition of a new final status code ought to specify whether or not it  
 is heuristically cacheable. Note that any response with a final status code   can 
be cached if the response has explicit freshness information. A status   code defined 
as heuristically cacheable is allowed to be cached without   explicit freshness information. 
  Likewise, the definition of a status code can place   constraints upon cache behavior 
if the must-understand cache   directive is used. See [[CACHING](#section-7.7 "text-left")] 
for more information.[¶](#section-7.7 "text-left")

   Finally, the definition of a new status code ought to indicate whether the   content 
has any implied association with an identified resource ([Section 6.4.2](#section-7.8 "text-left")).[¶](#section-7.8 "text-left")

        
### [16.3. ](#section-8 "text-left")[Field Extensibility](#section-8 "text-left")        
  HTTP's most widely used extensibility point is the definition of new header and 
 trailer fields.[¶](#section-8.1 "text-left")

  New fields can be defined such that, when they are understood by a  recipient, 
they override or enhance the interpretation of previously  defined fields, define 
preconditions on request evaluation, or  refine the meaning of responses.[¶](#section-8.2 "text-left")

   However, defining a field doesn't guarantee its deployment or recognition   by 
recipients. Most fields are designed with the expectation that a recipient   can 
safely ignore (but forward downstream) any field not recognized.   In other cases, 
the sender's ability to understand a given field might be   indicated by its prior 
communication, perhaps in the protocol version   or fields that it sent in prior 
messages, or its use of a specific media type.   Likewise, direct inspection of support 
might be possible through an   OPTIONS request or by interacting with a defined well-known 
URI   [[RFC8615](#name-media-type "text-left")] if such inspection is defined along 
with   the field being introduced.[¶](#section-8.3.2 "text-left")

          
#### [16.3.1. ](#section-8.3.3 "text-left")[Field Name Registry](#section-8.3.3 "text-left")          
   The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Field Name Registry" defines the   namespace 
for HTTP field names.[¶](#section-8.4 "text-left")

   Any party can request registration of an HTTP field. See [Section 16.3.2](#section-8.4 "text-left") for considerations to take   into account when creating a new HTTP field.[¶](#section-8.4.1 "text-left")

   The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Field Name Registry" is located at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-fields/](#section-8.4.1
"text-left")>.   Registration requests can be made by following the instructions 
located   there or by sending an email to the "ietf-http-wg@w3.org" mailing list.[¶](#section-8.4.1.1 "text-left")

   Field names are registered on the advice of a designated expert   (appointed by 
the IESG or their delegate). Fields with the status   'permanent' are Specification 
Required   ([[RFC8126](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")], [Section 4.6](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")).[¶](#name-deflate-coding "text-left")

   Registration requests consist of the following information:[¶](#section-8.4.1.3 "text-left")
            Field name:                The requested field name. It MUST conform 
to the    field-name syntax defined in [Section 5.1](#section-8.5 "text-left"), and it SHOULD 
be    restricted to just letters, digits, and hyphen ('-')    characters, with the 
first character being a letter.[¶](#section-8.5.1 "text-left")            Status: 
               "permanent", "provisional", "deprecated", or "obsoleted".[¶](#section-8.6 "text-left") 
           Specification document(s):                Reference to the document that 
specifies    the field, preferably including a URI that can be used to retrieve  
  a copy of the document. Optional but encouraged for provisional registrations. 
   An indication of the relevant section(s) can also be included, but is not required.[¶](#section-8.8 "text-left") 
         
   And optionally:[¶](#section-8.8 "text-left")
            Comments:                Additional information, such as about reserved 
entries.[¶](#section-8.8.1 "text-left")          
   The expert(s) can define additional fields to be collected in the   registry, 
in consultation with the community.[¶](#section-8.8.2 "text-left")

   Standards-defined names have a status of "permanent". Other names can also   be 
registered as permanent if the expert(s) finds that they are in use, in   consultation 
with the community. Other names should be registered as   "provisional".[¶](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")

   Provisional entries can be removed by the expert(s) if -- in consultation   with 
the community -- the expert(s) find that they are not in use. The   expert(s) can 
change a provisional entry's status to permanent at any time.[¶](#section-8.8.2.2 "text-left")

   Note that names can be registered by third parties (including the   expert(s)) 
if the expert(s) determines that an unregistered name is widely   deployed and not 
likely to be registered in a timely manner otherwise.[¶](#section-8.8.3 "text-left")

          
#### [16.3.2. ](#section-8.8.3.1 "text-left")[Considerations for New Fields](#section-8.8.3.1 "text-left")          
   HTTP header and trailer fields are a widely used extension point for the protocol. 
  While they can be used in an ad hoc fashion, fields that are intended for   wider 
use need to be carefully documented to ensure interoperability.[¶](#name-comparison-2 "text-left")

   In particular, authors of specifications defining new fields are advised to consider 
  and, where appropriate, document the following aspects:[¶](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left")

- Under what conditions the field can be used; e.g., only in     responses or requests, in all messages, only on responses to a     particular request method, etc.[¶](#section-9 "text-left")
            - Whether the field semantics are further refined by their context,     such as their use with certain request methods or status codes.[¶](#section-9.1 "text-left")
            - The scope of applicability for the information conveyed.     By default, fields apply only to the message they are     associated with, but some response fields are designed to apply to all     representations of a resource, the resource itself, or an even broader     scope. Specifications that expand the scope of a response field will     need to carefully consider issues such as content negotiation, the time     period of applicability, and (in some cases) multi-tenant server     deployments.[¶](#section-9.2.1 "text-left")
            - Under what conditions intermediaries are allowed to insert,     delete, or modify the field's value.[¶](#section-9.2.2 "text-left")
            - If the field is allowable in trailers; by     default, it will not be (see [Section 6.5.1](#section-9.2.3 "text-left")).[¶](#section-9.2.3 "text-left")
            - Whether it is appropriate or even required to list the field name in the     [Connection](#name-methods-and-caching "text-left") header field (i.e., if the field is to     be hop-by-hop; see [Section 7.6.1](#section-9.3 "text-left")).[¶](#section-9.3 "text-left")
            - Whether the field introduces any additional security considerations, such     as disclosure of privacy-related data.[¶](#section-9.3.1 "text-left")
          

   Request header fields have additional considerations that need to be documented 
  if the default behavior is not appropriate:[¶](#section-9.3.2 "text-left")

- If it is appropriate to list the field name in a     [Vary](#section-9.3.2 "text-left") response header field (e.g., when the request header     field is used by an origin server's content selection algorithm; see     [Section 12.5.5](#section-9.3.3 "text-left")).[¶](#section-9.3.3 "text-left")
            - If the field is intended to be stored when received in a PUT   request (see [Section 9.3.4](#section-9.3.4 "text-left")).[¶](#section-9.3.4 "text-left")
            - If the field ought to be removed when automatically redirecting a   request due to security concerns (see [Section 15.4](#section-9.3.5 "text-left")).[¶](#section-9.3.5 "text-left")
          

            
##### [16.3.2.1. ](#section-9.3.6 "text-left")[Considerations for New Field Names](#section-9.3.6 "text-left")            
   Authors of specifications defining new fields are advised to choose a short   
but descriptive field name. Short names avoid needless data transmission;   descriptive 
names avoid confusion and "squatting" on names that might have   broader uses.[¶](#section-9.3.8 "text-left")

   To that end, limited-use fields (such as a header confined to a single   application 
or use case) are encouraged to use a name that includes that use   (or an abbreviation) 
as a prefix; for example, if the Foo Application needs   a Description field, it 
might use "Foo-Desc"; "Description" is too generic,   and "Foo-Description" is needlessly 
long.[¶](#name-message-context "text-left")

   While the field-name syntax is defined to allow any token character, in   practice 
some implementations place limits on the characters they accept   in field-names. 
To be interoperable, new field names SHOULD constrain   themselves to alphanumeric 
characters, "-", and ".", and SHOULD   begin with a letter. For example, the underscore 
  ("_") character can be problematic when passed through non-HTTP   gateway interfaces 
(see [Section 17.10](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

   Field names ought not be prefixed with "X-"; see   [[BCP178](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")] 
for further information.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

   Other prefixes are sometimes used in HTTP field names; for example,   "Accept-" 
is used in many content negotiation headers, and "Content-" is used   as explained 
in [Section 6.4](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css"). These prefixes are   only an aid to recognizing the purpose of a field and do not   trigger automatic processing.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

            
##### [16.3.2.2. ](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")[Considerations for New Field Values](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")            
   A major task in the definition of a new HTTP field is the specification of   the 
field value syntax: what senders should generate, and how recipients   should infer 
semantics from what is received.[¶](rfc9110.xml "rfc-local.css")

   Authors are encouraged (but not required) to use either the ABNF rules in   this 
specification or those in [[RFC8941](#copyright "rfc-local.css")] to define the syntax 
  of new field values.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Authors are advised to carefully consider how the combination of multiple   field 
lines will impact them (see [Section 5.3](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")). Because   senders might erroneously send multiple values, and both intermediaries   and HTTP libraries can perform combination automatically, this applies to   all field values -- even when only a single value is anticipated.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Therefore, authors are advised to delimit or encode values that contain   commas 
(e.g., with the [quoted-string](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") rule of   [Section 5.6.4](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), the String data type of  
[[RFC8941](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")], or a field-specific encoding).   This 
ensures that commas within field data are not confused   with the commas that delimit 
a list value.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   For example, the [Content-Type](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") field value only allows commas   inside quoted strings, which can be reliably parsed even when multiple   values are present. The [Location](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") field value provides a   counter-example that should not be emulated: because URIs can include   commas, it is not possible to reliably distinguish between a single value   that includes a comma from two values.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Authors of fields with a singleton value (see [Section 5.5](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")) are additionally advised to document how to treat   messages where the multiple members are present (a sensible default would   be to ignore the field, but this might not always be the right choice).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [16.4. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Authentication Scheme Extensibility](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        

          
#### [16.4.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Authentication Scheme Registry](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Authentication Scheme Registry"   defines 
the namespace for the authentication schemes in challenges and   credentials. It 
is maintained   at <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-authschemes](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")>.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
