  Registrations MUST include the following fields:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

- Authentication Scheme Name[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Pointer to specification text[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Notes (optional)[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

  Values to be added to this namespace require IETF Review  (see [[RFC8126](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")], [Section 4.8](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [16.4.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Considerations for New Authentication Schemes](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
  There are certain aspects of the HTTP Authentication framework that put  constraints 
on how new authentication schemes can work:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

-                   HTTP authentication is presumed to be stateless: all of the information    necessary to authenticate a request MUST be provided in the request,    rather than be dependent on the server remembering prior requests.    Authentication based on, or bound to, the underlying connection is    outside the scope of this specification and inherently flawed unless    steps are taken to ensure that the connection cannot be used by any    party other than the authenticated user    (see [Section 3.3](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     The authentication parameter "realm" is reserved for defining protection      spaces as described in [Section 11.5](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"). New schemes      MUST NOT use it in a way incompatible with that definition.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     The "token68" notation was introduced for compatibility with existing      authentication schemes and can only be used once per challenge or credential.      Thus, new schemes ought to use the auth-param syntax instead, because      otherwise future extensions will be impossible.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     The parsing of challenges and credentials is defined by this specification      and cannot be modified by new authentication schemes. When the auth-param      syntax is used, all parameters ought to support both token and      quoted-string syntax, and syntactical constraints ought to be defined on      the field value after parsing (i.e., quoted-string processing). This is      necessary so that recipients can use a generic parser that applies to      all authentication schemes.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

	                        **Note:** The fact that the value syntax for the "realm" parameter      is restricted to quoted-string was a bad design choice not to be repeated      for new parameters.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     Definitions of new schemes ought to define the treatment of unknown      extension parameters. In general, a "must-ignore" rule is preferable      to a "must-understand" rule, because otherwise it will be hard to introduce      new parameters in the presence of legacy recipients. Furthermore,      it's good to describe the policy for defining new parameters (such      as "update the specification" or "use this registry").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     Authentication schemes need to document whether they are usable in      origin-server authentication (i.e., using [WWW-Authenticate](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")),      and/or proxy authentication (i.e., using [Proxy-Authenticate](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     The credentials carried in an [Authorization](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field are specific to      the user agent and, therefore, have the same effect on HTTP caches as the      "private" cache response directive ([Section 5.2.2.7](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")]),      within the scope of the request in which they appear.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

	      Therefore, new authentication schemes that choose not to carry      credentials in the [Authorization](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field (e.g., using a newly defined      header field) will need to explicitly disallow caching, by mandating the use of      cache response directives (e.g., "private").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            -                     Schemes using [Authentication-Info](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"), [Proxy-Authentication-Info](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css"),      or any other authentication related response header field need to      consider and document the related security considerations (see      [Section 17.16.4](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

        
### [16.5. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Range Unit Extensibility](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        

          
#### [16.5.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Range Unit Registry](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   The "HTTP Range Unit Registry" defines the namespace for the range   unit names 
and refers to their corresponding specifications.   It is maintained at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-parameters](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")>.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Registration of an HTTP Range Unit MUST include the following fields:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

- Name[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Description[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Pointer to specification text[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

  Values to be added to this namespace require IETF Review  (see [[RFC8126](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")], [Section 4.8](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [16.5.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Considerations for New Range Units](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   Other range units, such as format-specific boundaries like pages,   sections, 
records, rows, or time, are potentially usable in HTTP for   application-specific 
purposes, but are not commonly used in practice.   Implementors of alternative range 
units ought to consider how they would   work with content codings and general-purpose 
intermediaries.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [16.6. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Content Coding Extensibility](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        

          
#### [16.6.1. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Content Coding Registry](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   The "HTTP Content Coding Registry", maintained by   IANA at <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-parameters/](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")>,   registers [content-coding](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") names.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Content coding registrations MUST include the following fields:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

- Name[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Description[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
            - Pointer to specification text[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          

   Names of content codings MUST NOT overlap with names of transfer codings   (per 
the "HTTP Transfer Coding Registry" located at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-parameters/](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")>) unless   the encoding transformation is   identical (as is 
the case for the compression codings defined in   [Section 8.4.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")).[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Values to be added to this namespace require IETF Review   (see [Section 4.8](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[RFC8126](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]) and MUST   conform to the purpose of content coding defined in 
  [Section 8.4.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

          
#### [16.6.2. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Considerations for New Content Codings](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")          
   New content codings ought to be self-descriptive whenever possible, with   optional 
parameters discoverable within the coding format itself, rather   than rely on external 
metadata that might be lost during transit.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

        
### [16.7. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Upgrade Token Registry](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        
   The "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Upgrade Token Registry" defines   the 
namespace for protocol-name tokens used to identify protocols in the   [Upgrade](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") header field. The registry is maintained at  
<[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-upgrade-tokens](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")>.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Each registered protocol name is associated with contact information   and an 
optional set of specifications that details how the connection   will be processed 
after it has been upgraded.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   Registrations happen on a "First Come First Served" basis (see   [Section 4.4](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css") of [[RFC8126](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")]) and are subject to the   following rules:[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

               1. A protocol-name token, once registered, stays registered forever.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          2. A protocol-name token is case-insensitive and registered with the       preferred case to be generated by senders.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          3. The registration MUST name a responsible party for the       registration.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")
          4. The registration MUST name a point of contact.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          5. The registration MAY name a set of specifications associated with       that token. Such specifications need not be publicly available.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          6. The registration SHOULD name a set of expected "protocol-version"       tokens associated with that token at the time of registration.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          7. The responsible party MAY change the registration at any time.       The IANA will keep a record of all such changes, and make them       available upon request.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          8. The IESG MAY reassign responsibility for a protocol token.       This will normally only be used in the case when a       responsible party cannot be contacted.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
        

      
## [17. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[Security Considerations](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")      
   This section is meant to inform developers, information providers, and   users 
of known security concerns relevant to HTTP semantics and its   use for transferring 
information over the Internet. Considerations related   to caching are discussed 
in [Section 7](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") of [[CACHING](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")], 
  and considerations related to HTTP/1.1 message syntax and parsing are   discussed 
in [Section 11](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") of [[HTTP/1.1](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")].[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   The list of considerations below is not exhaustive. Most security concerns   related 
to HTTP semantics are about securing server-side applications (code   behind the 
HTTP interface), securing user agent processing of content   received via HTTP, or 
secure use of the Internet in general, rather than   security of the protocol. The 
security considerations for URIs, which   are fundamental to HTTP operation, are 
discussed in   [Section 7](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") of [[URI](rfc-local.css
"section-abstract")]. Various organizations maintain   topical information and links 
to current research on Web application   security (e.g., [[OWASP](rfc-local.css
"section-abstract")]).[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

        
### [17.1. ](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")[Establishing Authority](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")         
               
   HTTP relies on the notion of an "authoritative response": a   response that has 
been determined by (or at the direction of) the origin   server identified within 
the target URI to be the most appropriate response   for that request given the state 
of the target resource at the time of   response message origination.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   When a registered name is used in the authority component, the "http" URI   scheme 
([Section 4.2.1](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")) relies on the user's local name   resolution service to determine where it can find authoritative responses.   This means that any attack on a user's network host table, cached names,   or name resolution libraries becomes an avenue for attack on establishing   authority for "http" URIs. Likewise, the user's choice of server for   Domain Name Service (DNS), and the hierarchy of servers from which it   obtains resolution results, could impact the authenticity of address   mappings; DNS Security Extensions (DNSSEC,
[[RFC4033](urn:issn:2070-1721 "section-abstract")]) are   one way to improve authenticity, 
as are the various mechanisms for making   DNS requests over more secure transfer 
protocols.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

   Furthermore, after an IP address is obtained, establishing authority for   an 
"http" URI is vulnerable to attacks on Internet Protocol routing.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")

   The "https" scheme ([Section 4.2.2](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110 "section-abstract")) is intended to prevent   (or at least reveal) many of these potential attacks on establishing   authority, provided that the negotiated connection is secured and   the client properly verifies that the communicating server's identity   matches the target URI's authority component   ([Section 4.3.4](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2818 "section-abstract")). Correctly implementing such verification   can be difficult (see
[[Georgiev](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2818 "section-abstract")]).[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7230 "section-abstract")

   Authority for a given origin server can be delegated through protocol   extensions; 
for example, [[ALTSVC](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7232 "section-abstract")]. 
Likewise, the set of   servers for which a connection is considered authoritative 
can be changed   with a protocol extension like [[RFC8336](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7538
"section-abstract")].[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7615 "section-abstract")

   Providing a response from a non-authoritative source, such as a shared   proxy 
cache, is often useful to improve performance and availability, but   only to the 
extent that the source can be trusted or the distrusted   response can be safely 
used.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

   Unfortunately, communicating authority to users can be difficult.   For example, 
"phishing" is an attack on the user's perception   of authority, where that perception 
can be misled by presenting similar   branding in hypertext, possibly aided by userinfo 
obfuscating the authority   component (see [Section 4.2.1](#abstract "section-abstract")).   User agents can reduce the impact of phishing attacks by enabling users to   easily inspect a target URI prior to making an action, by prominently   distinguishing (or rejecting) userinfo when present, and by not sending   stored credentials and cookies when the referring document is from an   unknown or untrusted source.[¶](#abstract "section-abstract")

        
### [17.2. ](#section-abstract-1 "text-left")[Risks of Intermediaries](#section-abstract-1 "text-left")        
   HTTP intermediaries are inherently situated for on-path attacks.   Compromise 
of   the systems on which the intermediaries run can result in serious security  
 and privacy problems. Intermediaries might have access to security-related   information, 
personal information about individual users and   organizations, and proprietary 
information belonging to users and   content providers. A compromised intermediary, 
or an intermediary   implemented or configured without regard to security and privacy 
  considerations, might be used in the commission of a wide range of   potential 
attacks.[¶](#name-status-of-this-memo "text-left")

   Intermediaries that contain a shared cache are especially vulnerable   to cache 
poisoning attacks, as described in [Section 7](#section-boilerplate.1-1 "text-left") of [[CACHING](#section-boilerplate.1-1
"text-left")].[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")

   Implementers need to consider the privacy and security   implications of their 
design and coding decisions, and of the   configuration options they provide to operators 
(especially the   default configuration).[¶](#section-boilerplate.1-2 "text-left")

   Intermediaries are no more trustworthy than the people and policies   under which 
they operate; HTTP cannot solve this problem.[¶](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110 "text-left")

        
### [17.3. ](https://www.rfc-editor.org/info/rfc9110 "text-left")[Attacks Based on File and Path Names](#section-boilerplate.1-3 "text-left")        
   Origin servers frequently make use of their local file system to manage the   
mapping from target URI to resource representations.   Most file systems are not 
designed to protect against malicious file   or path names. Therefore, an origin 
server needs to avoid accessing   names that have a special significance to the system 
when mapping the   target resource to files, folders, or directories.[¶](https://trustee.ietf.org/license-info "text-left")

   For example, UNIX, Microsoft Windows, and other operating systems use ".."   as 
a path component to indicate a directory level above the current one,   and they 
use specially named paths or file names to send data to system devices.   Similar 
naming conventions might exist within other types of storage   systems. Likewise, 
local storage systems have an annoying tendency to   prefer user-friendliness over 
security when handling invalid or unexpected   characters, recomposition of decomposed 
characters, and case-normalization   of case-insensitive names.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-2 "text-left")

   Attacks based on such special names tend to focus on either denial-of-service 
  (e.g., telling the server to read from a COM port) or disclosure   of configuration 
and source files that are not meant to be served.[¶](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")

        
### [17.4. ](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")[Attacks Based on Command, Code, or Query Injection](#section-boilerplate.2-3 "text-left")        
   Origin servers often use parameters within the URI as a   means of identifying 
system services, selecting database entries, or   choosing a data source. However, 
data received in a request cannot be   trusted. An attacker could construct any of 
the request data elements   (method, target URI, header fields, or content) to contain 
data that might   be misinterpreted as a command, code, or query when passed through 
a   command invocation, language interpreter, or database interface.[¶](#name-table-of-contents "text-left")

   For example, SQL injection is a common attack wherein additional query   language 
is inserted within some part of the target URI or header   fields (e.g., [Host](#section-1 "text-left"), [Referer](#section-1 "text-left"), etc.).   If the received data is used directly within a SELECT statement, the   query language might be interpreted as a database command instead of a   simple string value. This type of implementation vulnerability is extremely   common, in spite of being easy to prevent.[¶](#name-purpose "text-left")

   In general, resource implementations ought to avoid use of request data   in contexts 
that are processed or interpreted as instructions.  Parameters   ought to be compared 
to fixed strings and acted upon as a result of that   comparison, rather than passed 
through an interface that is not prepared   for untrusted data. Received data that 
isn't based on fixed parameters   ought to be carefully filtered or encoded to avoid 
being misinterpreted.[¶](#section-1.3 "text-left")

   Similar considerations apply to request data when it is stored and later   processed, 
such as within log files, monitoring tools, or when included   within a data format 
that allows embedded scripts.[¶](#name-specifications-obsoleted-by "text-left")

        
### [17.5. ](#section-2 "text-left")[Attacks via Protocol Element Length](#section-2 "text-left")        
   Because HTTP uses mostly textual, character-delimited fields, parsers are   often 
vulnerable to attacks based on sending very long (or very slow)   streams of data, 
particularly where an implementation is expecting a   protocol element with no predefined 
length   ([Section 2.3](#section-2.2 "text-left")).[¶](#section-2.2 "text-left")

   To promote interoperability, specific recommendations are made for minimum   size 
limits on fields ([Section 5.4](#section-2.3 "text-left")). These are   minimum recommendations, chosen to be supportable even by implementations   with limited resources; it is expected that most implementations will   choose substantially higher limits.[¶](#section-2.4 "text-left")

   A server can reject a message that   has a target URI that is too long ([Section 15.5.15](#section-2.4 "text-left")) or request content   that is too large ([Section 15.5.14](#section-2.5 "text-left")). Additional status codes related to   capacity limits have been defined by extensions to HTTP  
[[RFC6585](#section-2.5 "text-left")].[¶](#name-protocol-version "text-left")

   Recipients ought to carefully limit the extent to which they process other   protocol 
elements, including (but not limited to) request methods, response   status phrases, 
field names, numeric values, and chunk lengths.   Failure to limit such processing 
can result in arbitrary code execution due to   buffer or arithmetic   overflows, 
and increased vulnerability to denial-of-service attacks.[¶](#section-3.1 "text-left")

        
### [17.6. ](#section-3.2 "text-left")[Attacks Using Shared-Dictionary Compression](#section-3.2 "text-left")        
   Some attacks on encrypted protocols use the differences in size created by   dynamic 
compression to reveal confidential information; for example, [[BREACH](#section-3.3 "text-left")]. 
These attacks rely on creating a redundancy between   attacker-controlled content 
and the confidential information, such that a   dynamic compression algorithm using 
the same dictionary for both content   will compress more efficiently when the attacker-controlled 
content matches   parts of the confidential content.[¶](#section-3.5 "text-left")

   HTTP messages can be compressed in a number of ways, including using TLS   compression, 
content codings, transfer codings, and other extension or   version-specific mechanisms.[¶](#section-3.6 "text-left")

   The most effective mitigation for this risk is to disable compression on   sensitive 
data, or to strictly separate sensitive data from attacker-controlled   data so that 
they cannot share the same compression dictionary. With   careful design, a compression 
scheme can be designed in a way that is not   considered exploitable in limited use 
cases, such as HPACK ([[HPACK](#name-intermediaries "text-left")]).[¶](#section-3.8 "text-left")

        
### [17.7. ](#section-3.8 "text-left")[Disclosure of Personal Information](#section-3.9 "text-left")        
   Clients are often privy to large amounts of personal information,   including 
both information provided by the user to interact with resources   (e.g., the user's 
name, location, mail address, passwords, encryption   keys, etc.) and information 
about the user's browsing activity over   time (e.g., history, bookmarks, etc.). 
Implementations need to   prevent unintentional disclosure of personal information.[¶](#section-4.1 "text-left")

        
### [17.8. ](#name-uri-references "text-left")[Privacy of Server Log Information](#section-4.2 "text-left")        
   A server is in the position to save personal data about a user's requests   over 
time, which might identify their reading patterns or subjects of   interest.  In 
particular, log information gathered at an intermediary   often contains a history 
of user agent interaction, across a multitude   of sites, that can be traced to individual 
users.[¶](#name-http-uri-scheme "text-left")

   HTTP log information is confidential in nature; its handling is often   constrained 
by laws and regulations.  Log information needs to be securely   stored and appropriate 
guidelines followed for its analysis.   Anonymization of personal information within 
individual entries helps,   but it is generally not sufficient to prevent real log 
traces from being   re-identified based on correlation with other access characteristics. 
  As such, access traces that are keyed to a specific client are unsafe to   publish 
even if the key is pseudonymous.[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")

   To minimize the risk of theft or accidental publication, log information   ought 
to be purged of personally identifiable information, including   user identifiers, 
IP addresses, and user-provided query parameters,   as soon as that information is 
no longer necessary to support operational   needs for security, auditing, or fraud 
control.[¶](#section-4.2.5 "text-left")

        
### [17.9. ](#section-4.3 "text-left")[Disclosure of Sensitive Information in URIs](#section-4.3 "text-left")        
   URIs are intended to be shared, not secured, even when they identify secure   
resources. URIs are often shown on displays, added to templates when a page   is 
printed, and stored in a variety of unprotected bookmark lists.   Many servers, proxies, 
and user agents log or display the target URI   in places where it might be visible 
to third parties.   It is therefore unwise to include information within a URI that 
  is sensitive, personally identifiable, or a risk to disclose.[¶](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")

   When an application uses client-side mechanisms to construct a target URI   out 
of user-provided information, such as the query fields of a form using   GET, potentially 
sensitive data might be provided that would not be   appropriate for disclosure within 
a URI. POST is often preferred in such   cases because it usually doesn't construct 
a URI; instead, POST of a form   transmits the potentially sensitive data in the 
request content. However, this   hinders caching and uses an unsafe method for what 
would otherwise be a safe   request. Alternative workarounds include transforming 
the user-provided data   prior to constructing the URI or filtering the data to only 
include common   values that are not sensitive. Likewise, redirecting the result 
of a query   to a different (server-generated) URI can remove potentially sensitive 
data   from later links and provide a cacheable response for later reuse.[¶](#name-ip-id-reference-identity "text-left")

   Since the [Referer](#section-5 "text-left") header field tells a target site about the   context that resulted in a request, it has the potential to reveal   information about the user's immediate browsing history and any personal   information that might be found in the referring resource's URI.   Limitations on the Referer header field are described in [Section 10.1.3](#section-5.1 "text-left") to   address some of its security considerations.[¶](#name-field-names "text-left")

        
### [17.10. ](#section-5.2 "text-left")[Application Handling of Field Names](#name-field-lines-and-combined-fi "text-left")        
   Servers often use non-HTTP gateway interfaces and frameworks to process a received 
  request and produce content for the response. For historical reasons, such interfaces 
  often pass received field names as external variable names, using a name mapping 
  suitable for environment variables.[¶](#section-5.4 "text-left")

   For example, the Common Gateway Interface (CGI) mapping of protocol-specific  
 meta-variables, defined by [Section 4.1.18](#section-5.5 "text-left") of [[RFC3875](#section-5.5
"text-left")],   is applied to received header fields that do not correspond to 
one of CGI's   standard variables; the mapping consists of prepending "HTTP_" to 
each name   and changing all instances of hyphen ("-") to underscore ("_"). This 
same mapping   has been inherited by many other application frameworks in order to 
simplify   moving applications from one platform to the next.[¶](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")

   In CGI, a received [Content-Length](#section-5.6.1 "text-left") field would be passed   as the meta-variable "CONTENT_LENGTH" with a string value matching the   received field's value. In contrast, a received "Content_Length" header field would   be passed as the protocol-specific meta-variable "HTTP_CONTENT_LENGTH",   which might lead to some confusion if an application mistakenly reads the   protocol-specific meta-variable instead of the default one. (This historical practice   is why [Section 16.3.2.1](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left") discourages the creation   of new field names that contain an underscore.)[¶](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")

   Unfortunately, mapping field names to different interface names can lead to   
security vulnerabilities if the mapping is incomplete or ambiguous. For example, 
  if an attacker were to send a field named "Transfer_Encoding", a naive interface 
  might map that to the same variable name as the "Transfer-Encoding" field, resulting 
  in a potential request smuggling vulnerability ([Section 11.2](#name-tokens "text-left") of [[HTTP/1.1](#section-5.6.3
"text-left")]).[¶](#section-5.6.3 "text-left")

   To mitigate the associated risks, implementations that perform such   mappings 
are advised to make the mapping unambiguous and complete   for the full range of 
potential octets received as a name (including those   that are discouraged or forbidden 
by the HTTP grammar).   For example, a field with an unusual name character might 
  result in the request being blocked, the specific field being removed,   or the 
name being passed with a different prefix to distinguish it from   other fields.[¶](#section-5.6.5 "text-left")

        
### [17.11. ](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")[Disclosure of Fragment after Redirects](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")        
   Although fragment identifiers used within URI references are not sent   in requests, 
implementers ought to be aware that they will be visible to   the user agent and 
any extensions or scripts running as a result of the   response. In particular, when 
a redirect occurs and the original request's   fragment identifier is inherited by 
the new reference in   [Location](#section-6 "text-left") ([Section 10.2.2](#section-6 "text-left")), this might   have the effect of disclosing one site's fragment to another site.   If the first site uses personal information in fragments, it ought to   ensure that redirects to other sites include a (possibly empty) fragment   component in order to block that inheritance.[¶](#name-framing-and-completeness "text-left")

        
### [17.12. ](#section-6.2 "text-left")[Disclosure of Product Information](#section-6.2 "text-left")        
   The [User-Agent](#section-6.3 "text-left") ([Section 10.1.5](#section-6.3 "text-left")),   [Via](#name-header-fields "text-left") ([Section 7.6.3](#section-6.4 "text-left")), and   [Server](#section-6.4 "text-left") ([Section 10.2.4](#section-6.4 "text-left")) header fields often   reveal information about the respective sender's software systems.   In theory, this can make it easier for an attacker to exploit known   security holes; in practice, attackers tend to try all potential holes   regardless of the apparent software versions being used.[¶](#section-6.4.1 "text-left")

   Proxies that serve as a portal through a network firewall ought to take   special 
precautions regarding the transfer of header information that might   identify hosts 
behind the firewall. The [Via](#section-6.4.2 "text-left") header field   allows intermediaries to replace sensitive machine names with pseudonyms.[¶](#section-6.5 "text-left")

        
### [17.13. ](#section-6.5 "text-left")[Browser Fingerprinting](#section-6.5.1 "text-left")        
   Browser fingerprinting is a set of techniques for identifying a specific   user 
agent over time through its unique set of characteristics. These   characteristics 
might include information related to how it uses the underlying   transport protocol, 
  feature capabilities, and scripting environment, though of particular   interest 
here is the set of unique characteristics that might be   communicated via HTTP. 
Fingerprinting is considered a privacy concern   because it enables tracking of a 
user agent's behavior over time   ([[Bujlow](#section-6.6 "text-left")]) without 
  the corresponding controls that the user might have over other forms of   data 
collection (e.g., cookies). Many general-purpose user agents   (i.e., Web browsers) 
have taken steps to reduce their fingerprints.[¶](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")

   There are a number of request header fields that might reveal information   to 
servers that is sufficiently unique to enable fingerprinting.   The [From](#name-date "text-left") header field is the most obvious, though it is   expected that From will only be sent when self-identification is desired by   the user. Likewise, Cookie header fields are deliberately designed to   enable re-identification, so fingerprinting concerns only apply to   situations where cookies are disabled or restricted by the user agent's   configuration.[¶](#section-7 "text-left")

   The [User-Agent](#section-7 "text-left") header field might contain enough information   to uniquely identify a specific device, usually when combined with other   characteristics, particularly if the user agent sends excessive details   about the user's system or extensions. However, the source of unique   information that is least expected by users is   [proactive negotiation](#section-7.2 "text-left") ([Section 12.1](#section-7.2 "text-left")),   including the [Accept](#section-7.2 "text-left"), [Accept-Charset](#section-7.2 "text-left"),   [Accept-Encoding](#section-7.3 "text-left"), and [Accept-Language](#section-7.3 "text-left")   header fields.[¶](#section-7.3 "text-left")

   In addition to the fingerprinting concern, detailed use of the   [Accept-Language](#section-7.3.1 "text-left") header field can reveal information the   user might consider to be of a private nature. For example, understanding   a given language set might be strongly correlated to membership in a   particular ethnic group.   An approach that limits such loss of privacy would be for a user agent   to omit the sending of Accept-Language except for sites that have been   explicitly permitted, perhaps via interaction after detecting a [Vary](#name-to-a-proxy "text-left")   header field that indicates language negotiation might be useful.[¶](#section-7.3.3 "text-left")

   In environments where proxies are used to enhance privacy, user agents   ought 
to be conservative in sending proactive negotiation header fields.   General-purpose 
user agents that provide a high degree of header field   configurability ought to 
inform users about the loss of privacy that might   result if too much detail is 
provided. As an extreme privacy measure,   proxies could filter the proactive negotiation 
header fields in relayed   requests.[¶](#section-7.5 "text-left")

        
### [17.14. ](#section-7.5 "text-left")[Validator Retention](#section-7.6 "text-left")        
   The validators defined by this specification are not intended to ensure   the 
validity of a representation, guard against malicious changes, or   detect on-path 
attacks. At best, they enable more efficient cache   updates and optimistic concurrent 
writes when all participants are behaving   nicely. At worst, the conditions will 
fail and the client will receive a   response that is no more harmful than an HTTP 
exchange without conditional   requests.[¶](#section-7.6.2 "text-left")

   An entity tag can be abused in ways that create privacy risks. For example,   
a site might deliberately construct a semantically invalid entity tag that   is unique 
to the user or user agent, send it in a cacheable response with a   long freshness 
time, and then read that entity tag in later conditional   requests as a means of 
re-identifying that user or user agent. Such an   identifying tag would become a 
persistent identifier for as long as the   user agent retained the original cache 
entry. User agents that cache   representations ought to ensure that the cache is 
cleared or replaced   whenever the user performs privacy-maintaining actions, such 
as clearing   stored cookies or changing to a private browsing mode.[¶](#section-7.8 "text-left")

        
### [17.15. ](#section-7.8 "text-left")[Denial-of-Service Attacks Using Range](#section-8 "text-left")        
   Unconstrained multiple range requests are susceptible to denial-of-service   attacks 
because the effort required to request many overlapping ranges of   the same data 
is tiny compared to the time, memory, and bandwidth consumed   by attempting to serve 
the requested data in many parts.   Servers ought to ignore, coalesce, or reject 
egregious range requests, such   as requests for more than two overlapping ranges 
or for many small ranges   in a single set, particularly when the ranges are requested 
out of order   for no apparent reason. Multipart range requests are not designed 
to   support random access.[¶](#name-representation-metadata "text-left")

        
### [17.16. ](#section-8.3 "text-left")[Authentication Considerations](#section-8.3 "text-left")        
   Everything about the topic of HTTP authentication is a security   consideration, 
so the list of considerations below is not exhaustive.   Furthermore, it is limited 
to security considerations regarding the   authentication framework, in general, 
rather than discussing all of the   potential considerations for specific authentication 
schemes (which ought   to be documented in the specifications that define those schemes). 
  Various organizations maintain topical information and links to current   research 
on Web application security (e.g., [[OWASP](#section-8.3.3 "text-left")]),   including 
common pitfalls for implementing and using the authentication   schemes found in 
practice.[¶](#name-multipart-types "text-left")

          
#### [17.16.1. ](#section-8.4 "text-left")[Confidentiality of Credentials](#section-8.4 "text-left")          
   The HTTP authentication framework does not define a single mechanism for   maintaining 
the confidentiality of credentials; instead, each   authentication scheme defines 
how the credentials are encoded prior to   transmission. While this provides flexibility 
for the development of future   authentication schemes, it is inadequate for the 
protection of existing   schemes that provide no confidentiality on their own, or 
that do not   sufficiently protect against replay attacks. Furthermore, if the server 
  expects credentials that are specific to each individual user, the exchange   of 
those credentials will have the effect of identifying that user even if   the content 
within credentials remains confidential.[¶](#section-8.4.1.2 "text-left")

   HTTP depends on the security properties of the underlying transport- or   session-level 
connection to provide confidential transmission of   fields. Services that depend 
on individual user authentication require a   [secured](#section-8.4.1.3 "text-left") connection prior to exchanging credentials   ([Section 4.2.2](#name-gzip-coding "text-left")).[¶](#section-8.5 "text-left")

          
#### [17.16.2. ](#section-8.5 "text-left")[Credentials and Idle Clients](#section-8.5.1 "text-left")          
   Existing HTTP clients and user agents typically retain authentication   information 
indefinitely. HTTP does not provide a mechanism for the   origin server to direct 
clients to discard these cached credentials, since   the protocol has no awareness 
of how credentials are obtained or managed   by the user agent. The mechanisms for 
expiring or revoking credentials can   be specified as part of an authentication 
scheme definition.[¶](#section-8.7 "text-left")

   Circumstances under which credential caching can interfere with the   application's 
security model include but are not limited to:[¶](#name-content-location "text-left")

- Clients that have been idle for an extended period, following       which the server might wish to cause the client to re-prompt the       user for credentials.[¶](#name-validator-fields "text-left")
            - Applications that include a session termination indication       (such as a "logout" or "commit" button on a page) after which       the server side of the application "knows" that there is no       further reason for the client to retain the credentials.[¶](#section-8.8.2 "text-left")
          

   User agents that cache credentials are encouraged to provide a readily   accessible 
mechanism for discarding cached credentials under user control.[¶](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")

          
#### [17.16.3. ](#section-8.8.2.1 "text-left")[Protection Spaces](#name-generation "text-left")          
  Authentication schemes that solely rely on the "realm" mechanism for  establishing 
a protection space will expose credentials to all resources on  an origin server. 
Clients that have successfully made authenticated requests  with a resource can use 
the same authentication credentials for other  resources on the same origin server. 
This makes it possible for a different  resource to harvest authentication credentials 
for other resources.[¶](#name-etag "text-left")

  This is of particular concern when an origin server hosts resources for multiple 
 parties under the same origin ([Section 11.5](#section-8.8.3.1 "text-left")).  Possible mitigation strategies include restricting direct access to  authentication credentials (i.e., not making the content of the  [Authorization](#section-8.8.3.2 "text-left") request header field available), and separating protection  spaces by using a different host name (or port number) for each party.[¶](#name-comparison-2 "text-left")

          
#### [17.16.4. ](#section-8.8.3.3 "text-left")[Additional Response Fields](#name-example-entity-tags-varying "text-left")          
  Adding information to responses that are sent over an unencrypted  channel can 
affect security and privacy. The presence of the  [Authentication-Info](#section-9 "text-left") and [Proxy-Authentication-Info](#section-9 "text-left")  header fields alone indicates that HTTP authentication is in use. Additional  information could be exposed by the contents of the authentication-scheme  specific parameters; this will have to be considered in the definitions of these  schemes.[¶](#name-overview "text-left")

- Internet Engineering Task Force".[¶](#name-safe-methods "text-left")

### [18.4. ](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")[Field Name Registration](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")        
   This specification updates the HTTP-related aspects of the existing   registration 
procedures for message header fields defined in [[RFC3864](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")]. 
  It replaces the old procedures as they relate to HTTP by defining a new   registration 
procedure and moving HTTP field definitions into a separate   registry.[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   IANA has created a new registry titled "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP)    
Field Name Registry" as outlined in [Section 16.3.1](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css").[¶](rfc-local.css "rfc-local.css")

   IANA has moved all entries in the "Permanent Message Header Field   Names" and 
"Provisional Message Header Field Names" registries (see   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/message-headers/](rfc-local.css
"rfc-local.css")>) with the   protocol 'http' to this registry and has applied 
the following changes:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

               1. The 'Applicable Protocol' field has been omitted.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          2. Entries that had a status of 'standard', 'experimental', 'reserved', or   'informational' have been made to have a status of 'permanent'.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          3. Provisional entries without a status have been made to have a status of   'provisional'.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          4. Permanent entries without a status (after confirmation that the   registration document did not define one) have been made to have a status of   'provisional'. The expert(s) can choose to update the entries' status if there is   evidence that another is more appropriate.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
        

   IANA has annotated the "Permanent Message Header Field    Names" and "Provisional 
Message Header Field Names" registries with the    following note to indicate that 
HTTP field name registrations have moved:[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")
          
                  **Note**[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

    HTTP field name registrations have been moved to     [[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-fields](rfc-local.css
"section-abstract")] per     [RFC9110].[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")

   IANA has updated the "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Field Name Registry" 
  with the field names listed in the following table.[¶](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")


| [Table 9](rfc-local.css "section-abstract") |
||---------------------------|------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|------------|
| -                                           | -                         | -          | -                                                                   |
|                                             |
|                                             | Accept                    | permanent  | [12.5.1](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")                          |            |  |
|                                             | Accept-Charset            | deprecated | [12.5.2](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")                          |            |  |
|                                             | Accept-Encoding           | permanent  | [12.5.3](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")                          |            |  |
|                                             | Accept-Language           | permanent  | [12.5.4](rfc-local.css "section-abstract")                          |            |  |

                     The field name "\*" is reserved because using that name as  
 an HTTP header field might conflict with its special semantics in the   [Vary](#section-4.3.2 "text-left") header field ([Section 12.5.5](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")).[¶](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")

                                         IANA has updated the "Content-MD5" entry 
in the new registry to have   a status of 'obsoleted' with references to   [Section 14.15](#section-4.3.4 "text-left") of [[RFC2616](#section-4.3.4 "text-left")] 
(for the definition   of the header field) and   [Appendix B](#section-4.3.5 "text-left") of [[RFC7231](#section-4.3.5 "text-left")] 
(which removed the field   definition from the updated specification).[¶](#section-4.3.5 "text-left")

        
### [18.5. ](#section-5 "text-left")[Authentication Scheme Registration](#section-5 "text-left")        
   IANA has updated the   "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Authentication Scheme 
Registry"   at <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-authschemes](#section-5.1 "text-left")> 
with   the registration procedure of [Section 16.4.1](#section-5.2 "text-left").   No authentication schemes are defined in this document.[¶](#section-5.2 "text-left")

        
### [18.6. ](#section-5.3 "text-left")[Content Coding Registration](#section-5.3 "text-left")        
   IANA has updated the "HTTP Content Coding Registry" at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-parameters/](#section-5.4 "text-left")> 
  with the registration procedure of [Section 16.6.1](#name-field-limits "text-left")   and the content coding names summarized in the table below.[¶](#section-5.5 "text-left")


| [Table 10](#section-5.6 "text-left") |
||------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------|
| -                                    | -          | -                                                                                                                                                    |
|                                      |
|                                      | compress   | UNIX "compress" data format [[Welch](#name-lists-rule-abnf-extension "text-left")]                                                                   | [8.4.1.1](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")             |  |
|                                      | deflate    | "deflate" compressed data ([[RFC1951](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")]) inside      the "zlib" data format ([[RFC1950](#section-5.6.1.2 "text-left")]) | [8.4.1.2](#name-recipient-requirements "text-left") |  |
|                                      | gzip       | GZIP file format [[RFC1952](#section-5.6.2 "text-left")]                                                                                             | [8.4.1.3](#section-5.6.3 "text-left")               |  |
|                                      | identity   | Reserved                                                                                                                                             | [12.5.3](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")                |  |
|                                      | x-compress | Deprecated (alias for compress)                                                                                                                      | [8.4.1.1](#section-5.6.5 "text-left")               |  |
|                                      | x-gzip     | Deprecated (alias for gzip)                                                                                                                          | [8.4.1.3](#section-5.6.7 "text-left")               |  |

        
### [18.7. ](#section-6 "text-left")[Range Unit Registration](#section-6 "text-left")        
   IANA has updated the "HTTP Range Unit Registry" at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-parameters/](#name-message-abstraction "text-left")> 
  with the registration procedure of [Section 16.5.1](#section-6.1 "text-left")   and the range unit names summarized in the table below.[¶](#name-framing-and-completeness "text-left")


| [Table 11](#section-6.2 "text-left") |
||-----------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| -                                    | -               | -                                                                |
|                                      |
|                                      | bytes           | a range of octets                                                | [14.1.2](#name-content "text-left") |  |
|                                      | none            | reserved as keyword to indicate range requests are not supported | [14.3](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")  |  |

        
### [18.8. ](#section-6.5 "text-left")[Media Type Registration](#section-6.5 "text-left")        
   IANA has updated the "Media Types" registry at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/media-types](#name-trailer-fields "text-left")> 
  with the registration information in   [Section 14.6](#section-6.5.1 "text-left")   for the media type "multipart/byteranges".[¶](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left")

   IANA has updated the registry note about "q" parameters with   a link to [Section 12.5.1](#section-6.5.2 "text-left") of this document.[¶](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")

        
### [18.9. ](#section-6.6 "text-left")[Port Registration](#section-6.6 "text-left")        
   IANA has updated the "Service Name and Transport Protocol Port Number   Registry" 
at <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/service-names-port-numbers/](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")> 
  for the services on ports 80 and 443 that use UDP or TCP to:[¶](#section-6.6.1 "text-left")

               1. use this document as "Reference", and[¶](#section-6.6.2 "text-left")
          2. when currently unspecified, set "Assignee" to "IESG" and "Contact" to   "IETF_Chair".[¶](#section-7 "text-left")
        

        
### [18.10. ](#name-routing-http-messages "text-left")[Upgrade Token Registration](#section-7.1 "text-left")        
  IANA has updated the   "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Upgrade Token Registry" 
at   <[https://www.iana.org/assignments/http-upgrade-tokens](#name-determining-the-target-reso "text-left")> 
  with the registration procedure described in [Section 16.7](#section-7.2 "text-left")   and the upgrade token names summarized in the following table.[¶](#section-7.3 "text-left")

| [Table 12](#section-7.3 "text-left") |
||------|-----------------------------|-------------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| -                                    | -    | -                           | -                             |
|                                      |
|                                      | HTTP | Hypertext Transfer Protocol | any DIGIT.DIGIT (e.g., "2.0") | [2.5](#section-7.3.3 "text-left") |  |

[¶](#section-4.2.1 "text-left")


## [Appendix B. ](#section-4.2.2 "text-left")[Changes from Previous RFCs](#section-4.2.2 "text-left")      


### [B.1. ](#section-4.2.3 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 2818](#section-4.2.3 "text-left")        
None.[¶](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")


### [B.2. ](#section-4.2.4 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7230](#name-deprecation-of-userinfo-in- "text-left")        
The sections introducing HTTP's design goals, history, architecture,   conformance
criteria, protocol versioning, URIs, message routing, and   header fields have been
moved here.[¶](#name-https-references-with-fragm "text-left")

The requirement on semantic conformance has been replaced with permission to  
ignore or work around implementation-specific failures.    ([Section 2.2](#section-4.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-4.3 "text-left")

The description of an origin and authoritative access to origin servers has   
been extended for both "http" and "https" URIs to account for alternative   services
and secured connections that are not necessarily based on TCP.    (Sections [4.2.1](#name-uri-origin "text-left"), [4.2.2](#name-uri-origin "text-left"),   [4.3.1](#section-4.3.2 "text-left"), and [7.3.3](#section-4.3.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-4.3.2 "text-left")

Explicit requirements have been added to check the target URI scheme's semantics
and reject requests that don't meet any associated requirements.   ([Section 7.4](#section-4.3.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-4.3.3 "text-left")

Parameters in media type, media range, and expectation can be empty via   one
or more trailing semicolons.    ([Section 5.6.6](#section-4.3.4 "text-left"))[¶](#section-4.3.4 "text-left")

"Field value" now refers to the value after multiple field lines are combined
with commas -- by far the most common use. To refer to a single header   line's
value, use "field line value".   ([Section 6.3](#section-4.3.5 "text-left"))[¶](#section-4.3.5 "text-left")

Trailer field semantics now transcend the specifics of chunked transfer coding.
The use of trailer fields has been further limited to allow generation   as a trailer
field only when the sender knows the field defines that usage and   to allow merging
into the header section only if the recipient knows the   corresponding field definition
permits and defines how to merge. In all   other cases, implementations are encouraged
either to store the trailer   fields separately or to discard them instead of merging.
([Section 6.5.1](#section-5.1 "text-left"))[¶](#name-field-names "text-left")

The priority of the absolute form of the request URI over the Host   header field
by origin servers has been made explicit to align with proxy handling.   ([Section 7.2](#name-field-lines-and-combined-fi "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.3 "text-left")

The grammar definition for the Via field's "received-by" was   expanded in RFC
7230 due to changes in the URI grammar for host   [[URI](#section-5.3 "text-left")]
that are not desirable for Via. For simplicity,   we have removed uri-host from the
received-by production because it can   be encompassed by the existing grammar for
pseudonym. In particular, this   change removed comma from the allowed set of characters
for a host name in   received-by.   ([Section 7.6.3](#section-5.5 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.5 "text-left")


### [B.3. ](#name-field-values "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7231](#section-5.6 "text-left")        
Minimum URI lengths to be supported by implementations are now recommended.   
([Section 4.1](#section-5.6.1 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.1 "text-left")

The following have been clarified: CR and NUL in field values are to be rejected
or   mapped to SP, and leading and trailing whitespace needs to be   stripped from
field values before they are consumed.   ([Section 5.5](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.1.1 "text-left")

Parameters in media type, media range, and expectation can be empty via   one
or more trailing semicolons.   ([Section 5.6.6](#name-sender-requirements "text-left"))[¶](#name-sender-requirements "text-left")

An abstract data type for HTTP messages has been introduced to define the   components
of a message and their semantics as an abstraction across   multiple HTTP versions,
rather than in terms of the specific syntax form of   HTTP/1.1 in [[HTTP/1.1](#section-5.6.2
"text-left")], and reflect the contents after the   message is parsed. This makes
it easier to distinguish between requirements   on the content (what is conveyed)
versus requirements on the messaging   syntax (how it is conveyed) and avoids baking
limitations of early protocol   versions into the future of HTTP. ([Section 6](#section-5.6.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.3 "text-left")

The terms "payload" and "payload body" have been replaced with "content", to better
align with its usage elsewhere (e.g., in field names) and to avoid confusion   
with frame payloads in HTTP/2 and HTTP/3.   ([Section 6.4](#section-5.6.4 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.4 "text-left")

The term "effective request URI" has been replaced with "target URI".   ([Section 7.1](#section-5.6.5 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.5 "text-left")

Restrictions on client retries have been loosened to reflect implementation   
behavior.   ([Section 9.2.2](#section-5.6.6 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.6 "text-left")

The fact that request bodies on GET, HEAD, and DELETE are not interoperable has
been clarified.   (Sections [9.3.1](#name-parameters "text-left"), [9.3.2](#section-5.6.7 "text-left"), and [9.3.5](#section-5.6.7 "text-left"))[¶](#section-5.6.7 "text-left")

The use of the [Content-Range](#name-date-time-formats "text-left") header field   ([Section 14.4](#section-6 "text-left")) as a request modifier on PUT is allowed.   ([Section 9.3.4](#section-6 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6 "text-left")

A superfluous requirement about setting [Content-Length](#section-6.1 "text-left")   has been removed from the description of the OPTIONS method.   ([Section 9.3.7](#section-6.1 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.1 "text-left")

The normative requirement to use the "message/http" media type in   TRACE responses
has been removed.   ([Section 9.3.8](#section-6.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.2 "text-left")

List-based grammar for [Expect](#name-control-data "text-left") has been restored for compatibility with   RFC 2616.   ([Section 10.1.1](#section-6.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.3 "text-left")

[Accept](#name-header-fields "text-left") and [Accept-Encoding](#section-6.4 "text-left") are allowed in response   messages; the latter was introduced by
[[RFC7694](#section-6.4 "text-left")].   ([Section 12.3](#section-6.4 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.4.1 "text-left")

"Accept Parameters" (accept-params and accept-ext ABNF production) have   been
removed from the definition of the Accept field.   ([Section 12.5.1](#name-content-semantics "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")

The Accept-Charset field is now deprecated.   ([Section 12.5.2](#section-6.4.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.4.2 "text-left")

The semantics of "\*" in the [Vary](#section-6.5 "text-left") header field when other   values are present was clarified.   ([Section 12.5.5](#section-6.5 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.5 "text-left")

Range units are compared in a case-insensitive fashion.   ([Section 14.1](#section-6.5.1 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.5.1 "text-left")

The use of the Accept-Ranges field is not restricted to origin servers.   ([Section 14.3](#name-limitations-on-use-of-trail "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")

The process of creating a redirected request has been clarified.   ([Section 15.4](#section-6.5.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.5.2 "text-left")

Status code 308 (previously defined in [[RFC7538](#section-6.6 "text-left")])
has been added so that it's defined closer to status codes 301, 302, and 307.  
([Section 15.4.9](#section-6.6 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.6 "text-left")

Status code 421 (previously defined in   [Section 9.1.2](#section-6.6.1 "text-left") of [[RFC7540](#section-6.6.1
"text-left")]) has been added because of its general   applicability. 421 is no
longer defined as heuristically cacheable since   the response is specific to the
connection (not the target resource).   ([Section 15.5.20](#section-6.6.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-6.6.2 "text-left")

Status code 422 (previously defined in   [Section 11.2](#section-7 "text-left") of [[WEBDAV](#section-7
"text-left")]) has been added because of its general   applicability.   ([Section 15.5.21](#name-routing-http-messages "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.1 "text-left")


### [B.4. ](#section-7.1 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7232](#name-determining-the-target-reso "text-left")        
Previous revisions of HTTP imposed an arbitrary 60-second limit on the   determination
of whether Last-Modified was a strong validator to guard   against the possibility
that the Date and Last-Modified values are   generated from different clocks or at
somewhat different times during the   preparation of the response. This specification
has relaxed that to allow   reasonable discretion.   ([Section 8.8.2.2](#section-7.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.3.1 "text-left")

An edge-case requirement on If-Match and If-Unmodified-Since   has been removed
that required a validator not to be sent in a 2xx   response if validation fails
because the change request has already   been applied.   (Sections [13.1.1](#name-to-a-cache "text-left") and   [13.1.4](#section-7.3.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.3.2 "text-left")

The fact that If-Unmodified-Since does not apply to a resource without a   concept
of modification time has been clarified.   ([Section 13.1.4](#section-7.3.3 "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.3.3 "text-left")

Preconditions can now be evaluated before the request content is processed   rather
than waiting until the response would otherwise be successful.   ([Section 13.2](#section-7.4 "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.4 "text-left")


### [B.5. ](#section-7.5 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7233](#section-7.5 "text-left")        
Refactored the range-unit and ranges-specifier grammars to simplify   and reduce
artificial distinctions between bytes and other   (extension) range units, removing
the overlapping grammar of   other-range-unit by defining range units generically
as a token and   placing extensions within the scope of a range-spec (other-range).
This disambiguates the role of list syntax (commas) in all range sets,   including
extension range units, for indicating a range-set of more than   one range. Moving
the extension grammar into range specifiers also allows   protocol specific to byte
ranges to be specified separately.[¶](#name-connection "text-left")

It is now possible to define Range handling on extension methods.   ([Section 14.2](#section-7.6.2 "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.6.2 "text-left")

Described use of the [Content-Range](#section-7.6.3 "text-left") header field   ([Section 14.4](#section-7.6.3 "text-left")) as a request modifier to perform a   partial PUT.   ([Section 14.5](#name-via "text-left"))[¶](#section-7.7 "text-left")


### [B.6. ](#section-7.7 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7235](#name-message-transformations "text-left")        
None.[¶](#section-7.8 "text-left")


### [B.7. ](#section-8 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7538](#section-8 "text-left")        
None.[¶](#name-representation-data-and-met "text-left")


### [B.8. ](#section-8.1 "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7615](#section-8.1 "text-left")        
None.[¶](#section-8.2 "text-left")


### [B.9. ](#name-representation-metadata "text-left")[Changes from RFC 7694](#section-8.3 "text-left")        
This specification includes the extension defined in [[RFC7694](#section-8.3 "text-left")]
but leaves out examples and deployment considerations.[¶](#section-8.3.1 "text-left")
